### 19. Chlorhexidine digluconate - 4m (303) Solution 📝

**Chlorhexidine Digluconate: Substantive Antimicrobial Irrigant**

**Properties:**
- Concentration: 0.2-2.0% (2% most common in endodontics)
- pH: ~5.5-7.0 (less alkaline than NaOCl)
- Charge: Cationic (positively charged)

**Mechanisms of Action:**
```
├── Substantivity: Binds to dentin, releases slowly over hours/days
├── Membrane Disruption: Binds to bacterial cell walls → leakage → cell death
├── Biofilm Activity: Penetrates extracellular polymeric substance better than NaOCl
└── Broad Spectrum: Effective against Gram+, Gram-, fungi, some viruses
```

**Clinical Applications:**
```
├── Final Rinse: After NaOCl/EDTA to provide residual antimicrobial effect
├── NaOCl Alternative: For patients with NaOCl allergy/sensitivity
├── Persistent Infections: Adjunct for E. faecalis (less effective than Ca(OH)₂ but useful)
├── Regenerative Endodontics: Less cytotoxic to stem cells than NaOCl
└── Medicament Vehicle: CHX gel for inter-appointment disinfection
```

**Protocol:**
```
1. Sequence: Complete NaOCl irrigation → EDTA smear removal → Final CHX rinse (2-5mL × 1 min)
2. Activation: Sonic/ultrasonic agitation enhances dentin penetration
3. Drying: Blot canal; avoid saline rinse (inactivates CHX)
4. Obturation: Proceed immediately; CHX does not interfere with sealer setting
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Substantivity: Hours of residual antimicrobial action vs. minutes for NaOCl
├── Biocompatibility: Less cytotoxic to periapical tissues than NaOCl
├── Stability: No degradation with time/light exposure
├── Taste: Less unpleasant than NaOCl for patient comfort
```

**Limitations:**
```
├── No Tissue Dissolution: Cannot replace NaOCl for organic debris removal
├── Precipitation Risk: Forms brown precipitate with NaOCl; always rinse with saline between
├── Inactivation: Anionic compounds (soaps, some sealers) reduce efficacy
├── Staining: Potential for tooth discoloration with prolonged exposure
└── Limited Evidence: Less long-term outcome data vs. NaOCl-based protocols
```

**Evidence-Based Position:**
- CHX is valuable adjunct but not replacement for NaOCl in primary irrigation
- Best used as final rinse for substantivity in high-risk cases (persistent infection, immunocompromised)
- Emerging role in regenerative endodontics due to stem cell compatibility

*Clinical Pearl:* Always use saline rinse between NaOCl and CHX to prevent precipitate formation; CHX complements but does not substitute for NaOCl's tissue dissolution [[303]]

---

### 20. Endovac - 2m (308) 📝

**EndoVac: Negative Pressure Irrigation System**

**Principle:** Uses apical negative pressure to draw irrigant to working length without extrusion risk.

**Components:**
```
├── Macro-cannula: Delivers irrigant to chamber
├── Micro-cannula: 32G tip placed to WL; creates suction to draw fluid apically
├── Vacuum source: Dental suction unit or dedicated pump
└── Control unit: Regulates flow rate, pressure
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Safety: Eliminates extrusion risk of NaOCl beyond apex
├── Efficacy: Irrigant reaches WL consistently, even in curved canals
├── Debris Removal: Negative pressure evacuates debris simultaneously
├── Efficiency: Reduces irrigation time vs. manual syringe techniques
```

**Protocol:**
1. Place micro-cannula to WL (confirmed radiographically)
2. Activate suction; irrigant flows from chamber to apex via negative pressure
3. Agitate with cannula motion; irrigate 20-30 sec per solution
4. Sequence: NaOCl → EDTA → Final NaOCl rinse

**Limitations:**
- Cost: ~$1000-1500 investment
- Learning curve: Proper cannula placement critical
- Not for viscous medicaments: Designed for liquid irrigants only

*Evidence:* EndoVac achieves superior apical debridement vs. syringe irrigation with 0% extrusion in controlled studies [[308]]

---

### 21. Cavit & IRM - 2m (313) 📝

**Cavit (Premixed Temporary Filling):**
```
├── Composition: Calcium sulfate, zinc oxide, glycol/glycol ester
├── Setting: Moisture-activated expansion; sets in 10-15 min
├── Advantages: Easy placement, good seal for short-term (1-2 weeks)
├── Limitations: Low strength; dissolves in saliva; not for long-term use
└── Clinical Use: Inter-appointment temporary seal; emergency temporary restoration
```

**IRM (Intermediate Restorative Material):**
```
├── Composition: Zinc oxide-eugenol with polymer reinforcement
├── Setting: Acid-base reaction; sets in 5-10 min, fully hardens in 24h
├── Advantages: Stronger than Cavit; antibacterial (eugenol); seals well for weeks
├── Limitations: Eugenol may interfere with resin bonding; not for permanent use
└── Clinical Use: Longer-term temporary (2-6 weeks); base under permanent restoration
```

**Selection Guide:**
- Cavit: Short inter-appointment intervals (<2 weeks), easy removal needed
- IRM: Longer intervals, need for strength/antibacterial effect, base for final restoration
- Both: Place minimum 3-4mm thickness; avoid contamination with sealer/irrigants

*Note: Neither is substitute for permanent restoration; schedule definitive restoration promptly*

---

### 22. Triple & double antibiotic paste - 4m (310) 📝

**Triple Antibiotic Paste (TAP):**
```
Composition:
├── Metronidazole (500mg): Anaerobic bacteria coverage
├── Ciprofloxacin (500mg): Gram-negative coverage
├── Minocycline (500mg): Broad-spectrum; causes tooth staining
├── Vehicle: Propylene glycol or macrogol ointment to form paste

Mechanism: Broad-spectrum antimicrobial action; disrupts bacterial cell walls, protein synthesis

Clinical Applications:
├── Regenerative Endodontics: Disinfect necrotic immature teeth prior to revascularization
├── Persistent Infections: Cases unresponsive to Ca(OH)₂ medicament
├── Trauma Cases: Avulsed/replanted teeth with high infection risk

Protocol:
1. Mix powders in equal parts with vehicle to thick paste
2. Place in canal with Lentulo spiral or paper point to WL
3. Seal access with Cavit/IRM; leave 2-4 weeks
4. Remove thoroughly before obturation (EDTA + ultrasonics)

Limitations:
├── Tooth Staining: Minocycline causes gray discoloration (especially in anterior teeth)
├── Antibiotic Resistance: Theoretical risk; use only when indicated
├── Cytotoxicity: High concentrations may harm stem cells in regenerative cases
└── Removal Difficulty: Paste adheres to dentin; requires thorough cleaning
```

**Double Antibiotic Paste (DAP):**
```
Composition:
├── Metronidazole + Ciprofloxacin (no minocycline)
├── Vehicle: Same as TAP

Advantages over TAP:
├── No Tooth Staining: Eliminates minocycline-related discoloration
├── Similar Antimicrobial Spectrum: For most endodontic pathogens
├── Better for Anterior Teeth: Where esthetics critical

Limitations:
├── Narrower Spectrum: Less effective against some Gram+ organisms
├── Still Antibiotic Concerns: Resistance potential, cytotoxicity at high concentrations

Clinical Selection:
├── Use DAP for anterior teeth, young patients where staining concern
├── Reserve TAP for posterior teeth, severe infections where broadest coverage needed
├── Always use lowest effective concentration (e.g., 1:1:1 diluted with vehicle)
```

**Evidence-Based Guidelines:**
- Antibiotic pastes are adjuncts to, not substitutes for, mechanical debridement
- Regenerative protocols: TAP/DAP for disinfection → blood clot induction → MTA coronal seal
- Success rates: 80-90% for regenerative cases with proper case selection
- Avoid routine use in primary RCT; reserve for specific indications

*Note: Emerging alternatives include calcium hydroxide + CHX combinations to avoid antibiotic concerns*

---

### 23. Resilon - 2m (318) 📝

**Resilon: Synthetic Polymer-Based Obturation Material**

**Composition:**
```
├── Resilon cones: Polycaprolactone polymer + radiopacifiers (bismuth oxychloride)
├── Self-etch primer: Bonds to dentin and Resilon
├── Resin sealer: Epiphany/RealSeal (methacrylate-based)
```

**Principle:** "Monoblock" concept – Resilon + sealer bond to dentin creating single unit.

**Advantages:**
```
├── Adhesion: Bonds to dentin and itself; potentially better seal than GP/sealer
├── Retreatment: Soluble in chloroform; easier removal than some thermoplasticized GP
├── Flexibility: Similar handling to gutta-percha
├── Radiopacity: Comparable to GP for verification
```

**Limitations:**
```
├── Technique Sensitivity: Requires strict moisture control for bonding
├── Long-Term Data: Less clinical evidence vs. gutta-percha (decades of use)
├── Cost: More expensive than traditional GP/sealer systems
├── Polymer Degradation: Theoretical concerns about long-term stability
```

**Clinical Status:**
- Initial enthusiasm for monoblock concept; subsequent studies show mixed outcomes
- Not superior to well-executed GP/epoxy sealer obturation in systematic reviews
- Niche use: Cases where adhesion theoretically beneficial (resorption, perforation repair)

*Current Consensus:* Gutta-percha with epoxy/bioceramic sealer remains gold standard; Resilon an alternative with specific indications but not routine replacement

---

### 24. Thermafil Thermoplasticized tech - 4m (328) 📝

**Thermafil: Carrier-Based Thermoplasticized Gutta-Percha System**

**Components:**
```
├── Carrier: Flexible plastic or metal shaft pre-coated with α-phase GP
├── Oven: Heats carrier to 160-200°C for GP plasticization
├── Sizing: Carriers match ISO instrument tapers (0.02, 0.04, 0.06)
└── Accessories: Carrier cutter, pluggers for coronal adaptation
```

**Technique:**
```
1. Canal Preparation:
   ├── Prepare to size matching carrier taper (e.g., 0.04 taper for ProTaper F4 carrier)
   ├── Verify working length; ensure apical stop
   └── Dry canal; apply thin sealer layer

2. Carrier Heating:
   ├── Place carrier in oven per manufacturer time/temperature
   ├── Test GP flow on gauze before intraoral use
   └── Avoid overheating (degrades GP, damages carrier)

3. Insertion:
   ├── Insert heated carrier to WL in single smooth motion
   ├── Maintain light apical pressure for 5-10 seconds during cooling
   └── Critical: Do not rotate carrier during insertion (risk of fracture)

4. Completion:
   ├── Sever carrier at orifice with heated instrument or cutter
   ├── Compact coronal GP with plugger; remove excess
   ├── Clean chamber; place immediate temporary restoration
   └── Radiographic verification of adaptation, length
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Efficiency: Fast obturation (30-60 sec per canal)
├── Predictability: Consistent GP adaptation with minimal technique variation
├── Adaptation: Flows into irregularities due to thermoplasticized GP
├── Ease of Use: Less technique-sensitive than continuous wave/backfill
└── Versatility: Plastic carriers flexible for curved canals; metal for straight
```

**Limitations & Risk Management:**
```
├── Carrier Fracture:
│   ├── Prevention: Avoid rotation during insertion; use gentle pressure
│   └── Management: If fractured, attempt retrieval with ultrasonics; if apical, may leave if sealed
│
├── Extrusion Risk:
│   ├── Prevention: Adequate apical stop; verify WL meticulously
│   └── Management: Monitor asymptomatic extrusions; surgical removal if symptomatic
│
├── Retreatment Difficulty:
│   ├── Challenge: Carrier + GP mass harder to remove than cold GP
│   └── Solution: Use solvents (chloroform), ultrasonics, specialized retreatment files
│
├── Cost: Carrier cost per tooth higher than cones; oven investment
│   └── ROI: Time savings may justify cost for high-volume practices
│
└── Learning Curve:
    ├── Practice on extracted teeth to master heating/insertion timing
    └── Standardize protocol for consistent results
```

**Clinical Indications:**
- Straight to moderately curved canals
- High-volume practices where efficiency critical
- Cases where predictable adaptation prioritized over retreatment ease
- Teaching: Simplifies obturation for students after mastering fundamentals

**Contraindications:**
- Severely curved canals (>40°): Risk of carrier fracture, transportation
- Immature teeth with open apices: Extrusion risk without apical barrier
- Cases with high retreatment likelihood: Carrier complicates removal
- Thin root walls: Compaction forces may risk fracture

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
- Success rates: 90-95% comparable to other warm techniques when properly indicated
- Adaptation: Superior to cold lateral compaction in oval/complex canals
- Long-term  5-10 year studies show comparable outcomes to GP/epoxy systems

*Best Practice:* Thermafil is a tool, not a technique substitute; success depends on proper case selection, canal preparation, and coronal seal – not just obturation method [[328]]

---

### 25. AH 26, AH Plus - 2m (335) 📝

**AH 26 vs. AH Plus: Epoxy Resin Sealers**

| Feature | AH 26 | AH Plus |
|---------|-------|---------|
| **Composition** | Epoxy resin + silver powder + bismuth oxide | Epoxy resin + zirconium oxide + calcium tungstate |
| **Radiopacifier** | Bismuth oxide + silver | Zirconium oxide + calcium tungstate |
| **Color** | Gray (silver content) | Light beige (no silver) |
| **Tooth Staining** | Significant gray discoloration risk | Minimal staining potential |
| **Setting Time** | 24-48 hours | 8-12 hours (faster) |
| **Solubility** | <1% (excellent) | <0.5% (superior) |
| **Adhesion** | Excellent to dentin/GP | Excellent; improved formulation |
| **Retreatment** | Difficult removal (strong bond) | Still difficult; slightly easier than AH 26 |
| **Biocompatibility** | Good; silver provides antimicrobial effect | Excellent; no silver cytotoxicity concerns |

**Clinical Selection:**
- AH Plus preferred for anterior teeth (esthetics), most contemporary cases
- AH 26 reserved for posterior teeth where staining irrelevant, or when silver antimicrobial effect desired
- Both: Mix powder:liquid per instructions; apply thin layer; proceed with obturation before initial set

*Note: AH Plus is updated formulation addressing AH 26 limitations; considered gold standard epoxy sealer in modern endodontics*

---

### 26. Ketac Endo, Activ GP - 2m (337) 📝

**Ketac Endo (Glass Ionomer Sealer):**
```
├── Composition: Fluoroaluminosilicate glass + polyacrylic acid
├── Advantages: Bonds chemically to dentin, fluoride release, biocompatible
├── Limitations: Brittle, technique-sensitive mixing, limited long-term data
└── Clinical Use: Niche applications; not first-line sealer in most practices
```

**Activ GP (Resin-Based System):**
```
├── Concept: GP cones coated with adhesive resin; used with resin sealer
├── Principle: "Monoblock" adhesion to dentin + GP
├── Advantages: Potential for superior seal via chemical bonding
├── Limitations: Technique-sensitive (moisture control), higher cost, limited outcome data
└── Clinical Status: Initial promise; not superior to well-executed epoxy/GP in systematic reviews
```

**Evidence Position:**
- Glass ionomer sealers: Biocompatible but inferior seal vs. epoxy/bioceramic in most studies
- Resin-based systems: Theoretical advantages not consistently translated to clinical superiority
- Current consensus: Epoxy (AH Plus) or bioceramic sealers with GP remain gold standard

*Note: Material selection should prioritize proven long-term outcomes over novel concepts without robust evidence*

---

### 27. Endorez, Diaket, Epiphany - 2m (338) 📝

**EndoREZ (Resin-Based Sealer):**
```
├── Composition: Hydrophilic methacrylate resin + hydroxyapatite
├── Claim: Penetrates dentinal tubules for "monoblock" seal
├── Reality: Technique-sensitive; moisture critical; mixed clinical outcomes
└── Status: Niche use; not widely adopted due to complexity vs. benefit
```

**Diaket (Polysulfide Sealer):**
```
├── Composition: Polysulfide polymer + lead dioxide (radiopacifier)
├── Properties: Good flow, adhesion; strong odor, staining potential
├── Limitations: Lead content concerns; largely superseded by epoxy/bioceramic
└── Status: Historical interest; rarely used in contemporary practice
```

**Epiphany (Resin Sealer for Resilon):**
```
├── Composition: Methacrylate-based sealer for Resilon obturation
├── Principle: Bonds to Resilon cones + dentin for monoblock
├── Limitations: Moisture sensitivity; no proven superiority over epoxy/GP
└── Status: Primarily of historical interest; Resilon/Epiphany system not widely used today
```

**Clinical Takeaway:**
- Novel sealer concepts (monoblock, tubule penetration) theoretically appealing
- However, systematic reviews show well-executed traditional techniques (GP + epoxy/bioceramic sealer) achieve equivalent or superior long-term success
- Prioritize evidence-based materials with proven outcomes over marketing claims

---

### 28. Sealapex, Apexit Plus - 2m (337) 📝

**Sealapex (Calcium Hydroxide Sealer):**
```
├── Composition: Calcium hydroxide + polymer matrix + radiopacifiers
├── Advantages: High pH antibacterial, promotes periapical healing, biocompatible
├── Limitations: Higher solubility than epoxy; lower seal quality in some studies
└── Clinical Use: Cases where biostimulation prioritized (immature teeth, resorption)
```

**Apexit Plus (Improved Calcium Hydroxide Sealer):**
```
├── Composition: Calcium hydroxide + radiopacifiers + polymer enhancers
├── Improvements: Lower solubility, better handling vs. original Sealapex
├── Advantages: Retains biocompatibility while improving physical properties
└── Clinical Position: Viable alternative when calcium hydroxide properties desired

Selection Guidance:
├── Use calcium hydroxide sealers when:
│   ├── Biostimulation needed (apexification, resorption repair)
│   ├── Patient has epoxy/amine sensitivity
│   └── Teaching environment (forgiving handling)
│
└── Prefer epoxy/bioceramic when:
    ├── Optimal seal critical (complex anatomy, retreatment)
    ├── Long-term predictability prioritized
    └── Evidence-based outcomes paramount
```

*Note: No sealer is universally ideal; match material properties to case-specific needs*

---

<a name="section-6"></a>
## 18-20: Prosto, Trauma, Endo-Perio

### 1. Management of root #s - 4m (384) 📝

**Root Fractures: Classification & Management**

**Classification (Andreasen):**
```
├── By Location:
│   ├── Cervical third: Poor prognosis; often requires extraction
│   ├── Middle third: Guarded prognosis; splinting + RCT possible
│   └── Apical third: Good prognosis; often heals with RCT alone
│
├── By Direction:
│   ├── Horizontal: Most common; prognosis depends on location
│   ├── Vertical: Poor prognosis; usually requires extraction
│   └── Oblique: Variable; management based on restorability
│
└── By Pulp Status:
    ├── Vital pulp: Splinting + monitoring; RCT only if necrosis develops
    └── Necrotic pulp: RCT of coronal segment; apical segment monitored
```

**Management Protocol:**

*Immediate Care (Emergency):*
```
1. Diagnosis:
   ├── Clinical: Mobility, pain to percussion, possible displacement
   ├── Radiographic: Multiple angled periapicals; CBCT for complex cases
   └── Vitality testing: Baseline for monitoring

2. Stabilization:
   ├── Flexible splint: Composite + wire/fiber for 2-4 weeks (cervical/middle fractures)
   ├── Occlusal adjustment: Eliminate traumatic forces on fractured tooth
   └── Soft diet: Minimize functional stress during healing

3. Pulp Management:
   ├── Vital pulp: Monitor vitality at 2, 4, 8, 12 weeks; RCT only if necrosis signs
   ├── Necrotic pulp: RCT of coronal segment; avoid over-instrumentation into fracture line
   └── Immature teeth: Consider regenerative approaches if pulp vital
```

*Definitive Treatment:*
```
Cervical Third Fractures:
├── If supragingival: Restore with composite/crown; monitor vitality
├── If subgingival: 
│   ├── Surgical crown lengthening if biologic width permits
│   ├── Orthodontic extrusion to expose fracture line
│   └── Extraction if non-restorable; plan for replacement

Middle Third Fractures:
├── Vital pulp: Splint 4 weeks; monitor; RCT only if necrosis develops
├── Necrotic pulp: RCT coronal segment; fill to fracture line; monitor apical segment
├── Healing: Calcific union (best), connective tissue interposition, or non-healing

Apical Third Fractures:
├── Often asymptomatic; discovered incidentally
├── Vital pulp: No treatment needed; monitor annually
├── Necrotic pulp: RCT of entire root if feasible; otherwise apicoectomy of coronal segment
└── Prognosis: Excellent with proper management; >85% long-term retention
```

*Vertical Root Fractures (Special Case):*
```
Diagnosis:
├── Clinical: Isolated deep pocket, J-shaped radiolucency, possible sinus tract
├── Radiographic: "Halo" lesion, fracture line if visible on CBCT
├── Confirmation: Surgical exploration, dye test, transillumination

Management:
├── Extraction: Usually required; poor prognosis for retention
├── Root amputation: Possible in multi-rooted teeth if fracture confined to one root
├── Prevention: Avoid excessive post preparation, conservative access design

Prognosis Factors:
├── Favorable: Apical location, vital pulp, timely stabilization, good oral hygiene
├── Unfavorable: Cervical location, necrotic pulp, infection, poor compliance
```

*Follow-up Protocol:*
```
├── Clinical: Symptoms, mobility, percussion, periodontal status at 2, 4, 8, 12 weeks, then 6-12 months
├── Radiographic: Healing assessment at 3, 6, 12 months; CBCT if uncertain
├── Long-term: Annual monitoring for late complications (resorption, infection)
```

*Evidence:* Horizontal root fractures have 70-90% long-term retention with proper management; vertical fractures >90% require extraction [[384]]

---

### 2. Walking bleach technique - 4m (461) 📝

**Definition:** Intracoronal bleaching of non-vital discolored teeth using sodium perborate placed in pulp chamber and sealed temporarily ("walking" between visits).

**Indications:**
- Discoloration of endodontically treated anterior teeth
- Intact enamel, no cervical defects or cracks
- Adequate root canal treatment with good apical seal

**Contraindications:**
- Cervical resorption, cracks, or defects (risk of bleach penetration)
- Inadequate root canal seal (risk of peroxide diffusion to periapex)
- Patient allergy to peroxide compounds

**Materials:**
```
├── Bleaching Agent: Sodium perborate monohydrate (preferred) or carbamide peroxide
├── Barrier: 2mm glass ionomer or resin-modified GIC over GP at CEJ
├── Temporary Seal: Cavit or IRM for access closure
├── Protective: Petroleum jelly on gingiva to prevent chemical burn
```

**Step-by-Step Protocol:**
```
1. Pre-Bleaching Assessment:
   ├── Confirm adequate RCT: Radiograph showing good obturation, no periapical pathology
   ├── Rule out cervical defects: Explorer, radiograph, transillumination
   ├── Shade documentation: Pre-op photos, shade guide recording

2. Access Preparation:
   ├── Remove existing restoration; access pulp chamber
   ├── Remove GP to 2mm below CEJ; place cervical barrier (glass ionomer)
   ├── Critical: Barrier prevents bleach penetration to periodontium (resorption risk)

3. Bleach Application:
   ├── Mix sodium perborate + distilled water/saline to thick paste
   ├── Pack into pulp chamber; avoid contact with barrier/tooth structure
   ├── Seal access with Cavit (3-4mm thickness); smooth margins

4. Waiting Period:
   ├── Leave 3-7 days; instruct patient to avoid staining foods/drinks
   ├── Monitor for sensitivity (rare with proper barrier)
   └── Return for evaluation; repeat if needed (max 3-4 applications)

5. Completion:
   ├── Remove bleach; irrigate chamber thoroughly
   ├── Etch, bond, place composite restoration matching adjacent teeth
   └── Final shade verification; document outcome
```

**Mechanism:** Sodium perborate releases hydrogen peroxide slowly; oxidizes chromogenic compounds (hemoglobin breakdown products) in dentin.

**Advantages:**
```
├── Safety: Lower concentration than in-office bleaching; minimal sensitivity
├── Efficacy: Gradual, controlled lightening; natural appearance
├── Cost: Inexpensive materials; minimal equipment
├── Predictability: >80% success in properly selected cases
```

**Complications & Prevention:**
```
├── External Cervical Resorption (Most Serious):
│   ├── Cause: Peroxide diffusion through dentinal tubules to periodontium
│   ├── Prevention: Always place 2mm cervical barrier; avoid in teeth with cracks/defects
│   └── Management: If occurs, stop bleaching; treat resorption surgically if possible
│
├── Inadequate Lightening:
│   ├── Cause: Insufficient applications, severe discoloration
│   ├── Prevention: Realistic expectations; consider veneer/crown if bleaching fails
│   └── Management: Additional applications (max 4) or restorative alternative
│
├── Sensitivity:
│   ├── Cause: Barrier failure, excessive concentration
│   ├── Prevention: Proper barrier technique; use sodium perborate (not high-concentration H₂O₂)
│   └── Management: Desensitizing agents; delay final restoration if needed
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
- Success rate: 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration with proper technique
- Long-term stability: 70-80% maintain shade at 5 years; touch-ups possible
- Safety: Cervical resorption risk <1% with proper barrier placement

*Clinical Pearl:* Walking bleach is first-line for non-vital tooth discoloration; always prioritize barrier placement over bleaching speed [[461]]

---

### 3. Endo-Perio lesions - 4m (401) 📝

**Definition:** Pathological communication between pulpal and periodontal tissues, creating diagnostic/therapeutic challenges.

**Classification (Simon et al.):**
```
1. Primary Endodontic Lesion:
   ├── Origin: Pulp necrosis → periapical inflammation → drainage via PDL
   ├── Features: Narrow probing depths except at sinus tract; vital adjacent teeth
   ├── Radiograph: Periapical radiolucency; possible lateral radiolucency along root
   └── Treatment: RCT alone; periodontal healing follows

2. Primary Periodontal Lesion:
   ├── Origin: Periodontal pocket → apical migration → pulp involvement via accessory canals
   ├── Features: Generalized periodontitis; deep probing; pulp may remain vital initially
   ├── Radiograph: Horizontal/vertical bone loss; possible periapical changes late
   └── Treatment: Periodontal therapy first; RCT only if pulp necrosis develops

3. Combined Lesion (Endo-Perio):
   ├── True Combined: Independent endo + perio lesions that merge
   │   ├── Features: Both periapical radiolucency + generalized bone loss
   │   └── Treatment: RCT + periodontal therapy; sequence based on symptoms
   │
   └── Endo-Perio Lesion: Endodontic infection draining through periodontal pocket
       ├── Features: Narrow deep pocket at sinus tract; otherwise healthy periodontium
       └── Treatment: RCT first; periodontal healing typically follows
```

**Diagnostic Approach:**
```
1. History: Onset of symptoms, dental/periodontal history, trauma
2. Clinical:
   ├── Pulp testing: Vitality of affected + adjacent teeth
   ├── Periodontal probing: Pattern (isolated deep pocket vs. generalized)
   ├── Mobility, sinus tract tracing (GP cone to source)
3. Radiographic:
   ├── Periapical + bitewing radiographs; CBCT if complex
   ├── Assess: Periapical status, bone loss pattern, root morphology
4. Differential Diagnosis:
   ├── Vertical root fracture, resorption, cyst, non-odontogenic pain
```

**Management Principles:**
```
Primary Endodontic Lesion:
├── RCT of affected tooth; eliminate infection source
├── Monitor periodontal healing: Pocket depth reduction, bone fill at 3-6 months
└── Periodontal therapy only if residual pockets after endodontic healing

Primary Periodontal Lesion:
├── Initial periodontal therapy: Scaling, root planing, oral hygiene instruction
├── Monitor pulp status: Vitality testing at 3-month intervals
├── RCT only if pulp necrosis develops (pain, discoloration, periapical changes)

Combined Lesion:
├── Sequence: Address most symptomatic component first (usually endodontic)
├── RCT: Eliminate pulpal infection; allow periapical healing
├── Periodontal therapy: 4-6 weeks post-RCT; scaling, possible surgery
├── Maintenance: 3-month recalls initially; monitor both tissues long-term

Critical Success Factors:
├── Accurate diagnosis: Misclassification leads to treatment failure
├── Patient compliance: Oral hygiene, recall attendance critical for periodontal component
├── Realistic expectations: Some bone loss may not fully regenerate; focus on stability
└── Interdisciplinary coordination: Endodontist + periodontist collaboration for complex cases
```

**Prognosis:**
```
├── Primary Endodontic: Excellent (>90% healing with proper RCT)
├── Primary Periodontal: Depends on periodontal severity; good with early intervention
├── Combined: Guarded; success requires addressing both components; 70-85% long-term retention
└── Poor Prognosis Indicators: Advanced bone loss, tooth mobility >II, furcation involvement Grade II-III
```

*Evidence:* Properly diagnosed/treated endo-perio lesions have 80-90% tooth retention at 5 years; misdiagnosis is leading cause of failure [[401]]

---

### 4. Classification of # teeth - 2m (376) Box 📝

**Ellis & Davey Classification of Crown Fractures:**
```
Class I: Fracture of enamel only; no dentin exposure
Class II: Fracture involving enamel + dentin; no pulp exposure
Class III: Fracture involving enamel + dentin + pulp exposure
Class IV: Traumatized tooth that becomes non-vital (with/without crown fracture)
Class V: Tooth avulsion (complete displacement from socket)
Class VI: Fracture of root with/without crown fracture
Class VII: Displacement of tooth without fracture of crown/root
Class VIII: Fracture of crown in its entirety
Class IX: Trauma to primary teeth (modified classification)
```

**Andreasen Classification (More Detailed):**
```
I. Hard Tissue Injuries:
   ├── Crown infraction (enamel crack)
   ├── Crown fracture (enamel, enamel-dentin, complicated with pulp exposure)
   ├── Crown-root fracture
   └── Root fracture (cervical, middle, apical third)

II. Periodontal Tissue Injuries:
   ├── Concussion, subluxation
   ├── Extrusive/lateral luxation
   ├── Intrusion
   └── Avulsion

III. Supporting Bone Injuries:
   ├── Socket wall fracture
   ├── Alveolar process fracture
   └── Jaw fracture

IV. Gingival/Oral Mucosa Injuries:
   ├── Laceration, contusion, abrasion
   └── Avulsion of gingiva
```

*Clinical Use:* Ellis classification quick reference for crown fractures; Andreasen comprehensive for all dental trauma documentation/research

---

### 5. Vertical root #'s - 4m (135) 📝

**Definition:** Longitudinal fracture of root starting from canal and extending toward periodontium; often iatrogenic or due to excessive occlusal forces.

**Etiology:**
```
├── Iatrogenic:
│   ├── Excessive post preparation (removing too much dentin)
│   ├── Over-instrumentation during RCT (transportation, thinning walls)
│   ├── Excessive condensation forces during obturation
│   └── Improper access design weakening root structure
│
├── Functional:
│   ├── Excessive occlusal forces (bruxism, malocclusion)
│   ├── Large restorations compromising tooth integrity
│   └── Parafunctional habits (nail-biting, pipe smoking)
│
└── Material Factors:
    ├── Rigid posts (metal) vs. flexible (fiber)
    ├── Inadequate ferrule (<2mm) increasing fracture risk
    └── Aging dentin becoming more brittle
```

**Clinical Features:**
```
├── Symptoms: 
│   ├── Often asymptomatic initially; discovered on routine radiograph
│   ├── Possible: Pain on biting, isolated deep pocket, sinus tract
│   └── Late: Mobility, swelling, tooth discoloration
│
├── Signs:
│   ├── Isolated narrow probing defect (often buccal)
│   ├── "J-shaped" or "halo" radiolucency around root apex
│   ├── Possible fracture line visible on CBCT (not always on 2D radiographs)
│   └── Tooth may test non-vital (if pulp necrotic) or vital (if fracture recent)
│
├── Diagnostic Challenges:
    ├── Mimics periodontal abscess, failed RCT, cyst
    └── Requires high index of suspicion in endodontically treated teeth with new symptoms
```

**Diagnosis:**
```
1. Clinical:
   ├── Periodontal probing: Isolated deep pocket at fracture site
   ├── Transillumination: Fiber-optic light may reveal fracture line
   ├── Dye test: Methylene blue applied to cleaned root surface
   ├── Bite test: Pain on release may indicate fracture
2. Radiographic:
   ├── Multiple angled periapicals: Fracture line occasionally visible
   ├── CBCT: Gold standard for detection (sensitivity ~85%); shows fracture plane
3. Surgical:
   ├── Flap reflection: Direct visualization of fracture line
   └── Histopathology: Confirmation if extracted
```

**Management:**
```
Prevention (Most Critical):
├── Conservative access design: Preserve dentin per "laws of access"
├── Appropriate post selection: Fiber posts for flexibility; adequate ferrule (≥2mm)
├── Occlusal management: Night guard for bruxers; adjust traumatic contacts
├── Patient education: Avoid hard foods with restored endodontically treated teeth

Treatment Options (Limited):
├── Extraction: Usually required; poor prognosis for retention
│   ├── Socket preservation graft if implant planned
│   └── Discuss replacement options (implant, bridge, partial)
│
├── Root Amputation (Multi-rooted teeth only):
│   ├── If fracture confined to one root; other roots healthy
│   ├── Procedure: Surgical removal of fractured root + RCT of remaining roots
│   └── Prognosis: Guarded; requires excellent oral hygiene, regular maintenance
│
├── Intentional Replantation (Experimental):
│   ├── Extract tooth, repair fracture extraorally with bonding/resin, replant
│   ├── High risk of ankylosis, resorption; limited success data
│   └── Consider only for highly motivated patients with no alternatives

Prognosis:
├── Overall: Poor; >90% of vertical root fractures require extraction
├── Favorable factors: Early detection, single-rooted tooth, good bone support
└── Unfavorable: Multi-rooted tooth with furcation involvement, advanced bone loss
```

**Evidence-Based Insights:**
- Incidence: 2-5% of endodontically treated teeth; higher in molars, teeth with posts
- Risk factors: Post placement increases risk 3-5×; inadequate ferrule doubles risk
- Prevention focus: Conservative dentistry, proper case selection, patient education most effective strategies

*Clinical Pearl:* Suspect vertical root fracture in any endodontically treated tooth with new isolated deep pocket or "J-shaped" radiolucency; early diagnosis prevents unnecessary RCT retreatment [[135]]

---

### 6. Management of avulsed teeth - 4m (392) 📝

**Definition:** Complete displacement of tooth from socket due to trauma.

**Critical Factor:** Time out of socket and storage medium determine prognosis.

**Emergency Management (At Accident Site):**
```
1. Immediate Replantation (Ideal):
   ├── Handle tooth by crown only; avoid touching root
   ├── Rinse gently with saline/milk if dirty (do NOT scrub)
   ├── Replant immediately into socket; stabilize with finger pressure
   └── Seek dental care within 30 minutes

2. If Replantation Not Possible:
   ├── Storage Media (Best to Worst):
   │   ├── Hank's Balanced Salt Solution (HBSS): Gold standard; preserves PDL cells
   │   ├── Milk (cold, preferably whole): Readily available; good for <3 hours
   │   ├── Saliva (buccal vestibule): Better than dry; risk of swallowing
   │   ├── Saline: Acceptable short-term; no nutrients for PDL
   │   └── Water: Hypotonic; damages PDL cells; avoid if possible
   │
   ├── Dry Time:
   │   ├── <15 minutes: Excellent prognosis with prompt care
   │   ├── 15-60 minutes: Good prognosis if stored properly
   │   ├── >60 minutes dry: Poor prognosis; PDL cells non-viable
   │   └── >2 hours: Focus shifts to ankylosis management, not PDL preservation
```

**Dental Office Management:**
```
1. Assessment:
   ├── Medical history: Tetanus status, other injuries
   ├── Tooth evaluation: Root development (open/closed apex), fractures, contamination
   ├── Socket evaluation: Bone fractures, soft tissue lacerations

2. Replantation Protocol:
   ├── Anesthesia: Local infiltration; avoid vasoconstrictors if possible
   ├── Socket preparation: Gentle irrigation with saline; remove clot if organized
   ├── Tooth preparation:
   │   ├── If dry >60 min: Soak in 2% NaF for 20 min to slow resorption
   │   ├── Remove necrotic PDL gently with gauze (do NOT curette root)
   │   └── For open apex: Consider regenerative approach (see below)
   │
   ├── Replantation:
   │   ├── Insert tooth with gentle pressure; verify position radiographically
   │   ├── Flexible splint: Composite + wire/fiber for 7-14 days (longer if bone fracture)
   │   └── Occlusal adjustment: Eliminate contact in centric/protrusive
   │
   ├── Medications:
   │   ├── Antibiotics: Doxycycline 100mg BID × 7d (adults) or amoxicillin (children)
   │   ├── Tetanus prophylaxis: If contaminated wound, last booster >5 years ago
   │   └── Analgesics: NSAIDs for pain/inflammation
```

**Pulp Management Based on Root Development:**
```
Closed Apex (Mature Tooth):
├── RCT indicated: Initiate within 7-10 days post-replantation
├── Reason: Pulp revascularization unlikely; necrosis leads to infection/resorption
├── Technique: Gentle debridement; Ca(OH)₂ medicament × 4 weeks; obturate when asymptomatic

Open Apex (Immature Tooth):
├── Revascularization possible: Monitor vitality at 2, 4, 8 weeks
├── If vital: No RCT needed; continue monitoring for root development
├── If necrotic: 
│   ├── Traditional: Apexification with Ca(OH)₂ or MTA plug
│   ├── Regenerative Endodontics (Preferred):
│   │   ├── Disinfect with low-concentration NaOCl + antibiotic paste (DAP)
│   │   ├── Induce bleeding into canal for stem cell scaffold
│   │   ├── Place MTA coronal barrier; restore permanently
│   │   └── Goal: Continue root development, thicken walls, reduce fracture risk
```

**Follow-up Protocol:**
```
├── Clinical: 
│   ├── 2 weeks: Splint removal; assess mobility, symptoms, periodontal status
│   ├── 3, 6, 12 months: Vitality testing (if open apex), percussion, probing
│   └── Annual: Long-term monitoring for resorption, ankylosis
│
├── Radiographic:
│   ├── Baseline: Post-replantation periapical
│   ├── 3, 6, 12 months: Assess periapical healing, root development (if immature)
│   └── Annual: Monitor for replacement resorption (ankylosis), inflammatory resorption
│
└── Complications Monitoring:
    ├── Inflammatory Resorption: Rapid bone/tooth loss; treat with RCT + Ca(OH)₂
    ├── Replacement Resorption (Ankylosis): Tooth fuses to bone; infraocclusion over time
    └── Infection: Pain, swelling, sinus tract; requires RCT or extraction
```

**Prognosis Factors:**
```
Favorable:
├── Replantation <30 minutes; proper storage medium
├── Closed apex with timely RCT; open apex with regenerative approach
├── No root fracture, minimal contamination
└── Good oral hygiene, regular follow-up

Unfavorable:
├── Dry time >60 minutes; improper storage (water, dry)
├── Delayed RCT in mature teeth; missed regenerative window in immature teeth
├── Root fracture, severe contamination, immunocompromised patient
└── Poor compliance with follow-up, oral hygiene

Long-term Outcomes:
├── 5-year survival: 70-90% with optimal management
├── Ankylosis risk: 20-50% depending on dry time; higher in immature teeth
└── Esthetic/functional success: Depends on managing complications proactively
```

*Evidence-Based Guidelines:* IADT (International Association of Dental Traumatology) protocols provide standardized care; adherence improves outcomes by 30-50% [[392]]

---

### 7. Biological width - 2m (359) 📝

**Definition:** Combined dimension of junctional epithelium (~0.97mm) + connective tissue attachment (~1.07mm) = ~2.0mm of supracrestal tissue attachment.

**Clinical Significance:**
```
├── Restorative Margin Placement:
│   ├── Subgingival margins must respect biological width to avoid chronic inflammation
│   ├── Violation → gingival recession, bone loss, pocket formation
│   └── Rule: Margin ≥3mm from alveolar crest (2mm biological width + 1mm sulcus)
│
├── Endodontic Relevance:
│   ├── Cervical barrier placement during walking bleach: 2mm below CEJ to avoid resorption
│   ├── Post preparation: Avoid violating biological width during core buildup
│   └── Surgical access: Flap design must preserve supracrestal attachment
│
└── Management of Violation:
    ├── Orthodontic extrusion: Move tooth coronally to re-establish width
    ├── Surgical crown lengthening: Remove bone to create space for restoration
    └── Extraction: If violation severe and tooth non-restorable
```

*Note: Biological width is dynamic; individual variation exists, but 2mm is clinical guideline for treatment planning*

---

### 8. Ferrule - 2/4m (360) 📝

**Definition:** 360° collar of dentin encircling tooth coronal to finish line, providing fracture resistance for restored endodontically treated teeth.

**Ideal Ferrule:**
```
├── Height: ≥2mm of vertical dentin
├── Thickness: ≥1mm of dentin thickness
├── Circumference: Continuous 360° collar (no gaps)
├── Location: Coronal to finish line; within enamel/dentin (not cementum)
```

**Biomechanical Role:**
```
├── Stress Distribution: Ferrule absorbs occlusal forces, reducing stress on post/core
├── Fracture Resistance: Teeth with ≥2mm ferrule have 2-3× higher fracture resistance
├── Retention: Enhances crown retention; reduces debonding risk
└── Periodontal Health: Proper ferrule placement respects biological width
```

**Clinical Application:**
```
Assessment:
├── Pre-restoration: Evaluate remaining tooth structure radiographically/clinically
├── If inadequate ferrule:
│   ├── Crown lengthening surgery: Remove bone to expose more dentin
│   ├── Orthodontic extrusion: Move tooth coronally over weeks/months
│   └── Extraction: If insufficient structure despite interventions

Restoration Protocol:
├── Core buildup: Bonded composite or amalgam to create ferrule if needed
├── Post selection: Fiber post preferred; limit post length to root length/2
├── Crown design: Full coverage for posterior teeth; margin placement respecting biological width

Evidence: Teeth with ≥2mm ferrule have 90-95% 5-year survival vs. 50-60% without ferrule
```

*Clinical Pearl: Ferrule is more critical than post type for long-term success; prioritize tooth structure preservation during access/restoration*

---

### 9. Core - 4m (361) 📝

**Definition:** Foundation restoration replacing missing tooth structure to support final crown in endodontically treated teeth.

**Indications:**
- Extensive loss of coronal tooth structure post-RCT
- Need for retention form for final crown
- Replacement of temporary restoration with permanent foundation

**Materials Comparison:**

| Material | Advantages | Limitations | Best Use |
|----------|------------|-------------|----------|
| **Composite Resin** | Bonds to dentin, esthetic, conservative preparation | Technique-sensitive (moisture control), polymerization shrinkage | Anterior teeth, moderate structure loss |
| **Amalgam** | Strong, durable, forgiving technique | Non-adhesive, requires mechanical retention, esthetics | Posterior teeth, high occlusal load |
| **Glass Ionomer** | Bonds chemically, fluoride release, biocompatible | Lower strength, moisture sensitivity during placement | Base under composite, cervical areas |
| **Resin-Modified GIC** | Combines GIC bonding + composite strength | Still lower strength than composite alone | Moderate structure loss, moisture challenge |
| **Cast Metal Core** | Precise fit, strong for severely damaged teeth | Requires lab, tooth reduction, higher cost | Extreme structure loss; often with post |

**Core Buildup Protocol:**
```
1. Preparation:
   ├── Remove temporary restoration; clean chamber
   ├── Assess remaining dentin: Identify walls for retention
   ├── Etch/bond if using adhesive materials (composite, RMGIC)

2. Placement:
   ├── Composite: Incremental placement (2mm layers), light-cure each
   ├── Amalgam: Condense in layers; carve anatomy after setting
   ├── GIC/RMGIC: Mix per instructions; place before initial set
   └── Critical: Extend core to cover all dentin margins; create ferrule if possible

3. Finishing:
   ├── Shape core to provide adequate reduction for final crown
   ├── Verify margins: Smooth, continuous, respecting biological width
   └── Temporary crown: Protect core while final crown fabricated

4. Final Restoration:
   ├── Crown preparation: Minimal reduction to preserve core/dentin
   ├── Impression: Accurate margins for crown fit
   └── Cementation: Adhesive resin cement for composite cores; traditional for amalgam
```

**Critical Success Factors:**
```
├── Ferrule Creation: Core should extend to create ≥2mm ferrule if possible
├── Moisture Control: Rubber dam essential for adhesive materials
├── Bonding Protocol: Etch, prime, bond per manufacturer for composite success
├── Occlusal Design: Core anatomy should guide final crown preparation
└── Timing: Place permanent crown within 2-4 weeks to prevent core fracture
```

**Complications & Prevention:**
```
├── Core Fracture:
│   ├── Cause: Inadequate retention, excessive occlusal forces
│   ├── Prevention: Ferrule design, appropriate material selection, occlusal adjustment
│   └── Management: Replace core; assess need for post or crown redesign
│
├── Marginal Leakage:
│   ├── Cause: Poor adaptation, contamination during placement
│   ├── Prevention: Rubber dam isolation, proper bonding technique
│   └── Management: Replace core; address caries if present
│
├── Post/Core Debonding:
│   ├── Cause: Inadequate bonding, excessive forces
│   ├── Prevention: Follow bonding protocols; limit post length to root/2
│   └── Management: Re-cement if feasible; replace if failed
```

*Evidence:* Bonded composite cores with ferrule design show 90-95% 5-year survival; amalgam cores comparable in posterior teeth with adequate retention [[361]]

---

### 10. Factors determining Post selection - 4m (363) 📝

**Post Purpose:** Retain core foundation in endodontically treated teeth with extensive coronal loss.

**Key Selection Factors:**

```
1. Remaining Tooth Structure:
   ├── Adequate ferrule (≥2mm): May not need post; core alone sufficient
   ├── Minimal coronal structure: Post essential for core retention
   └── Root morphology: Curved roots favor flexible posts; straight roots accommodate rigid posts

2. Post Material:
   ├── Fiber Posts (Glass/Quartz):
   │   ├── Advantages: Esthetic, flexible (reduces root fracture risk), bondable
   │   ├── Limitations: Lower strength than metal; technique-sensitive bonding
   │   └── Best for: Anterior teeth, esthetic zones, curved roots
   │
   ├── Metal Posts (Stainless Steel, Titanium):
   │   ├── Advantages: High strength, predictable retention
   │   ├── Limitations: Rigid (stress concentration → root fracture), esthetic issues
   │   └── Best for: Posterior teeth with straight roots, high occlusal load
   │
   └── Custom Cast Posts:
       ├── Advantages: Precise fit for irregular canals
       ├── Limitations: Requires lab, tooth reduction, higher cost, rigid
       └── Best for: Severely damaged teeth with complex anatomy

3. Post Dimensions:
   ├── Length: Post length = crown length OR root length/2 (whichever greater); leave 4-5mm apical GP
   ├── Diameter: Post diameter ≤1/3 root diameter at any point to preserve dentin
   └── Taper: Match canal preparation; avoid excessive taper reducing retention

4. Cementation Protocol:
   ├── Adhesive Resin Cement:
   │   ├── Required for fiber posts; enhances retention, seals interface
   │   ├── Technique: Etch dentin, apply primer/bond, cement post immediately
   │   └── Critical: Moisture control; rubber dam essential
   │
   ├── Traditional Cements (Zinc Phosphate, GIC):
   │   ├── Used for metal posts; mechanical retention primary
   │   ├── Simpler technique; less moisture-sensitive
   │   └── Limitation: No adhesion; relies on post fit for retention
   │
   └── Self-Adhesive Resin Cements:
       ├── Balance of adhesion + ease of use
       ├── Good for fiber posts when moisture control challenging
       └── Slightly lower bond strength than total-etch resin cements

5. Occlusal Factors:
   ├── High occlusal load: Prefer stronger posts (metal) or avoid post if ferrule adequate
   ├── Parafunction (bruxism): Night guard essential regardless of post type
   └── Anterior guidance: Fiber posts preferred for esthetics + flexibility

6. Patient Factors:
   ├── Esthetic demands: Fiber posts for anterior teeth
   ├── Oral hygiene: Critical for long-term success regardless of post type
   ├── Compliance: Follow-up for monitoring core/crown integrity
   └── Cost considerations: Fiber posts + adhesive cement more expensive than metal + traditional cement
```

**Evidence-Based Guidelines:**
```
├── Ferrule is more critical than post type: Teeth with ≥2mm ferrule have 90-95% survival regardless of post
├── Fiber posts reduce root fracture risk vs. metal posts in curved roots (2-3× lower)
├── Post length: Longer posts increase retention but risk perforation; balance with apical GP seal
├── Adhesive cementation: Essential for fiber posts; improves retention 30-50% vs. traditional cements
└── Avoid posts when possible: If adequate coronal structure for core retention, post adds unnecessary risk
```

**Decision Algorithm:**
```
1. Assess remaining tooth structure:
   ├── Adequate ferrule + coronal walls → Core alone (no post)
   └── Minimal structure → Post indicated

2. Select post type:
   ├── Anterior/esthetic zone → Fiber post
   ├── Posterior/straight root → Metal or fiber based on load
   └── Complex anatomy → Custom cast post (rare)

3. Determine dimensions:
   ├── Length: Crown length or root/2 (leave 4-5mm apical GP)
   ├── Diameter: ≤1/3 root diameter at narrowest point
   └── Taper: Match canal preparation

4. Choose cement:
   ├── Fiber post → Adhesive resin cement (rubber dam mandatory)
   ├── Metal post → Traditional cement or self-adhesive resin
   └── Compromised isolation → Self-adhesive resin cement

5. Final restoration:
   ├── Core buildup with ferrule design
   ├── Full-coverage crown for posterior teeth
   └── Occlusal adjustment + night guard if parafunctional
```

*Clinical Pearl: Post is a means to retain core, not to strengthen tooth; prioritize ferrule design and conservative dentin preservation over post selection*

---

### 11. Glass fiber-reinforced epoxy resins - 2m (368) 📝

**Definition:** Fiber posts composed of glass fibers embedded in epoxy resin matrix; used for core retention in endodontically treated teeth.

**Properties:**
```
├── Composition: 60-70% glass fibers (E-glass, S-glass) + epoxy resin matrix
├── Radiopacity: Added barium/zirconium for radiographic visibility
├── Flexural Strength: 800-1200 MPa (comparable to dentin)
├── Modulus of Elasticity: 15-40 GPa (close to dentin's 18 GPa)
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Esthetics: Tooth-colored; ideal for anterior restorations
├── Biomechanical Compatibility: Flexibility similar to dentin → reduces stress concentration, root fracture risk
├── Adhesion: Bonds to resin cements via silane coupling agents
├── Retrievability: Can be removed with ultrasonics if retreatment needed
└── Biocompatibility: No metal ions; minimal tissue reaction
```

**Clinical Protocol:**
1. Post-space preparation: Remove GP to planned depth; avoid over-instrumentation
2. Try-in: Verify fit, length (leave 4-5mm apical GP)
3. Surface treatment: Silane application to enhance bonding
4. Cementation: Adhesive resin cement with total-etch or self-etch protocol
5. Core buildup: Bonded composite over post; shape for crown preparation

**Limitations:**
- Technique-sensitive bonding (moisture control critical)
- Lower strength than metal posts (avoid in high-load posterior cases without adequate ferrule)
- Cost: Higher than metal posts

*Evidence:* Fiber posts show 90-95% 5-year survival with proper case selection; superior to metal posts in reducing root fractures in curved roots [[368]]

---

<a name="section-7"></a>
## 21-24: Non Surgical Endodontics, Bleaching, Lasers & Surgery

### 1. Flap designs for endodontic surgery - 4m (434) 📝

**Purpose:** Provide access to root apex while preserving blood supply, minimizing scarring, allowing primary closure.

**Ideal Flap Characteristics:**
```
├── Adequate access: Visualize surgical field without excessive retraction
├── Blood supply: Base wider than free margin to preserve vasculature
├── Incision placement: Over sound bone, avoiding bony defects, mental foramen
├── Tissue handling: Sharp dissection, minimal trauma, tension-free closure
└── Esthetics: Incisions hidden in gingival sulcus where possible
```

**Common Flap Designs:**

| Flap Type | Design | Indications | Advantages | Limitations |
|-----------|--------|-------------|------------|-------------|
| **Submarginal Curved (Luebke-Ochsenbein)** | Semilunar incision 2-3mm apical to gingival margin | Anterior teeth with adequate attached gingiva | Esthetic (no gingival margin incision), simple | Limited access; risk of gingival recession if too close to margin |
| **Full-Thickness Triangular** | Sulcular incision + single vertical releasing incision | Most anterior/premolar surgeries | Good access, preserves papilla, predictable healing | Vertical incision may cause minor gingival recession |
| **Full-Thickness Rectangular** | Sulcular incision + two vertical releasing incisions | Molars, large lesions, complex anatomy | Excellent access, versatile for large defects | Two vertical incisions; slightly higher recession risk |
| **Papilla Preservation** | Modified sulcular incision preserving interdental papilla | Esthetic zone (anterior) surgeries | Maximizes papilla retention; optimal esthetics | Technically demanding; limited to specific anatomies |
| **Envelope Flap** | Sulcular incision without vertical releases | Small lesions, minimal access needed | Minimal tissue trauma; fast healing | Limited access; not for large defects |

**Surgical Protocol:**
```
1. Pre-op Planning:
   ├── Radiographs/CBCT: Assess lesion size, root anatomy, vital structures
   ├── Anesthesia: Local infiltration + nerve block; consider sedation for anxious patients
   ├── Antibiotics: Single pre-op dose if immunocompromised or large surgery

2. Flap Reflection:
   ├── Incision: Sharp blade; follow design precisely
   ├── Reflection: Full-thickness mucoperiosteal flap; use periosteal elevator gently
   ├── Retraction: Minimal pressure; moist gauze to prevent desiccation

3. Access & Procedure:
   ├── Osteotomy: Round bur with saline coolant to expose apex
   ├── Apicoectomy: 3mm root-end resection at 0-10° bevel
   ├── Retro-preparation: Ultrasonic tip for 3mm deep cavity
   ├── Retrofill: MTA/bioceramic material condensed into preparation
   └── Debridement: Curette granulation tissue; irrigate with saline

4. Closure:
   ├── Flap repositioning: Tension-free adaptation to bone
   ├── Suturing: 5-0 or 6-0 monofilament; interrupted or horizontal mattress
   ├── Hemostasis: Pressure, oxidized cellulose if needed
   └── Post-op instructions: Soft diet, oral hygiene modifications, analgesics

5. Post-op Care:
   ├── Suture removal: 5-7 days
   ├── Follow-up: Clinical/radiographic at 3, 6, 12 months
   └── Monitoring: Healing, complications (infection, dehiscence)
```

**Critical Success Factors:**
```
├── Flap Design Selection: Match to anatomy, lesion size, esthetic demands
├── Atraumatic Technique: Preserve blood supply; minimize tissue trauma
├── Magnification: Dental operating microscope enhances precision
├── Hemostasis: Clear field essential for retrofill adaptation
└── Tension-Free Closure: Prevents dehiscence, promotes primary healing
```

**Complications & Prevention:**
```
├── Gingival Recession:
│   ├── Cause: Incision too close to margin, excessive retraction
│   ├── Prevention: Place incisions in attached gingiva; gentle tissue handling
│   └── Management: Usually self-limiting; grafting if severe
│
├── Flap Dehiscence:
│   ├── Cause: Tension on closure, infection, poor blood supply
│   ├── Prevention: Tension-free suturing; antibiotics if indicated
│   └── Management: Secondary intention healing; possible revision surgery
│
├── Nerve Injury:
│   ├── Cause: Incision near mental foramen, deep osteotomy
│   ├── Prevention: Pre-op CBCT to identify vital structures; conservative osteotomy
│   └── Management: Usually transient; refer if persistent paresthesia
│
└── Infection:
    ├── Cause: Contamination, poor hemostasis, immunocompromise
    ├── Prevention: Aseptic technique; pre-op antibiotics for high-risk cases
    └── Management: Antibiotics; drainage if abscess forms
```

*Evidence:* Microsurgical endodontic surgery with modern flap designs achieves 90-95% success; flap selection is critical for esthetic and functional outcomes [[434]]

---

### 2. Root resection - 4m (438) 📝

**Definition:** Surgical removal of root portion (apicoectomy) or entire root (root amputation) to eliminate infection while retaining tooth.

**Types:**
```
I. Apicoectomy (Root-End Resection):
├── Procedure: Remove 3mm of root apex + periapical pathology
├── Indications: Persistent periapical lesion after adequate RCT; non-surgical retreatment not feasible
├── Key Steps: Flap design → osteotomy → apicoectomy → retro-preparation → retrofill → closure
└── Success: 90-95% with modern microsurgical techniques

II. Root Amputation:
├── Procedure: Remove entire root (usually one root of multi-rooted tooth)
├── Indications: Vertical root fracture, severe resorption, or untreatable canal in one root; other roots healthy
├── Key Steps: Flap → osteotomy → root sectioning → removal → contouring → closure
└── Success: 75-85% with proper case selection; requires excellent oral hygiene

III. Hemisection:
├── Procedure: Section multi-rooted tooth into halves; remove diseased half, restore remaining half
├── Indications: Mandibular molars with one root non-restorable; other root viable
├── Key Steps: Flap → tooth sectioning with bur → remove segment → RCT of retained root → restore as premolar
└── Success: 70-80%; functionally converts molar to premolar
```

**Apicoectomy: Detailed Protocol**
```
1. Pre-op:
   ├── Confirm non-surgical options exhausted: Adequate RCT, no coronal leakage
   ├── CBCT: Assess root anatomy, lesion size, proximity to vital structures
   ├── Patient consent: Discuss success rates, alternatives (extraction/implant)

2. Surgical Steps:
   ├── Flap: Full-thickness triangular/rectangular for access
   ├── Osteotomy: Round bur with saline to expose 3-4mm of apex
   ├── Apicoectomy: 
   │   ├── Resect 3mm of root at 0-10° bevel (minimizes dentinal tubule exposure)
   │   ├── Inspect resected surface for cracks, isthmuses, missed canals
   │   └── Stain with methylene blue to highlight anatomy
   │
   ├── Retro-preparation:
   │   ├── Ultrasonic tip (e.g., BUC series) to create 3mm deep cavity
   │   ├── Parallel to long axis; clean with EDTA/NaOCl irrigation
   │   └── Verify depth/direction under microscope
   │
   ├── Retrofill:
   │   ├── Material: MTA or bioceramic (superior seal/biocompatibility)
   │   ├── Technique: Condense with micro-pluggers; verify adaptation
   │   └── Radiograph: Confirm fill length, adaptation
   │
   └── Closure:
       ├── Debride socket; irrigate with saline
       ├── Reposition flap; suture tension-free with 6-0 monofilament
       └── Post-op: Analgesics, antibiotics if indicated; soft diet × 3 days

3. Post-op Care:
   ├── Suture removal: 5-7 days
   ├── Follow-up: Clinical/radiographic at 3, 6, 12, 24 months
   └── Success criteria: Asymptomatic, bone fill on radiograph, functional tooth
```

**Critical Success Factors:**
```
├── Case Selection: 
│   ├── Favorable: Small lesion, straight root, adequate coronal seal
│   ├── Unfavorable: Large lesion, curved root, poor oral hygiene
│
├── Magnification: Dental operating microscope essential for precision
├── Retrofill Material: MTA/bioceramic superior to amalgam/IRM in long-term studies
├── Bevel Angle: 0-10° minimizes dentinal tubule exposure vs. traditional 45° bevel
└── Coronal Seal: Address any coronal leakage pre-op; critical for long-term success
```

**Complications & Management:**
```
├── Persistent Infection:
│   ├── Cause: Incomplete debridement, missed canal, coronal leakage
│   ├── Prevention: Microscope use; thorough retro-preparation; seal coronal restoration
│   └── Management: Non-surgical retreatment first; repeat surgery if needed
│
├── Root Fracture:
│   ├── Cause: Excessive bevel, thin root walls, traumatic occlusion
│   ├── Prevention: Conservative resection; occlusal adjustment post-op
│   └── Management: Extraction if fractured; prevent with night guard if bruxism
│
├── Sinus Involvement (Maxillary posterior):
│   ├── Cause: Osteotomy extending into maxillary sinus
│   ├── Prevention: Pre-op CBCT; conservative osteotomy
│   └── Management: ENT consult if sinus communication; antibiotics
│
└── Nerve Injury (Mandibular posterior):
    ├── Cause: Osteotomy near inferior alveolar nerve
    ├── Prevention: CBCT planning; stay coronal to nerve canal
    └── Management: Usually transient; refer if persistent paresthesia
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
- Apicoectomy with MTA retrofill: 94% success at 5 years vs. 78% for amalgam
- Root amputation: 80% survival at 10 years with proper case selection, oral hygiene
- Microsurgical techniques improve success by 15-20% vs. traditional surgery

*Clinical Pearl: Apicoectomy is not "last resort" but predictable option when non-surgical retreatment not feasible; success depends on meticulous technique, case selection, and coronal seal*

---

### 3. Etiology of tooth discolouration - 4m (457) 📝

**Classification by Origin:**

```
I. Extrinsic Stains (Surface):
├── Dietary: Coffee, tea, red wine, berries (chromogens bind to pellicle)
├── Tobacco: Tar/nicotine deposits; brown/black stains
├── Medications: Chlorhexidine mouthrinse, iron supplements, liquid iron
├── Poor Oral Hygiene: Plaque/calculus accumulation; bacterial pigments
├── Occupational: Metal dust exposure (iron, copper)
└── Management: Professional prophylaxis, air-polishing, improved hygiene

II. Intrinsic Stains (Within Tooth Structure):
├── Developmental:
│   ├── Amelogenesis Imperfecta: Defective enamel formation; yellow-brown
│   ├── Dentinogenesis Imperfecta: Opalescent dentin; blue-gray/brown
│   ├── Fluorosis: Excess fluoride during development; white/brown mottling
│   ├── Tetracycline Staining: Antibiotic incorporation during tooth development; gray/brown bands
│   └── Management: Microabrasion, veneers, crowns depending on severity
│
├── Acquired:
│   ├── Pulp Necrosis: Hemoglobin breakdown products (hemosiderin, bilirubin) diffuse into dentin; gray/black discoloration
│   ├── Trauma: Pulp hemorrhage → similar to necrosis staining
│   ├── Internal Resorption: Vascular granulation tissue visible through enamel; pink hue
│   ├── Aging: Secondary dentin deposition; yellowing due to thinner enamel
│   ├── Restorative Materials: Amalgam tattoo, metal post diffusion; gray discoloration
│   └── Management: Vital bleaching (if vital), walking bleach (non-vital), restorative coverage
│
└── Iatrogenic:
    ├── Endodontic Materials: Sealer extrusion, GP with metal components; gray stains
    ├── Bleaching Complications: Over-bleaching → translucent, gray appearance
    └── Management: Prevention via careful technique; restorative correction if occurs

III. Combined Stains:
├── Extrinsic + Intrinsic: Common in long-standing discoloration
├── Example: Tetracycline-stained tooth with surface coffee stains
└── Management: Address intrinsic component first (bleaching), then extrinsic (prophylaxis)
```

**Endodontic-Specific Discoloration Mechanisms:**
```
├── Pulp Necrosis Pathway:
│   1. Trauma/caries → pulp hemorrhage
│   2. RBC breakdown → hemosiderin (brown), bilirubin (green), hematoidin (yellow)
│   3. Pigments diffuse through dentinal tubules to enamel
│   4. Result: Gray, brown, or pink discoloration visible clinically
│
├── Endodontic Material Staining:
│   ├── MTA: Bismuth oxide can cause gray discoloration if extruded coronally
│   ├── Gutta-Percha: Some formulations contain metal oxides causing gray hue
│   ├── Sealers: AH 26 (silver content) causes significant gray staining
│   └── Prevention: Place cervical barrier during walking bleach; use white MTA in anterior teeth
│
└── Post-Treatment Changes:
    ├── Dehydration: Temporary lightening immediately post-RCT; reverses as tooth rehydrates
    ├── Coronal Leakage: Microleakage allows bacterial pigments to stain dentin
    └── Prevention: Immediate permanent restoration after RCT; good marginal seal
```

**Diagnostic Approach:**
```
1. History: Onset, trauma, medications, dental treatment
2. Clinical:
   ├── Shade assessment: Compare to adjacent teeth; note pattern (generalized vs. localized)
   ├── Vitality testing: Determine if pulp vital/necrotic
   ├── Transillumination: Differentiate intrinsic vs. extrinsic; detect cracks
3. Radiographic:
   ├── Periapical: Assess periapical status, root development, restorations
   ├── Bitewing: Evaluate interproximal stains, caries
4. Differential Diagnosis:
   ├── Rule out: Caries, restoration margins, cracks mimicking discoloration
```

**Management Principles:**
```
├── Extrinsic: Prophylaxis, air-polishing, oral hygiene instruction
├── Intrinsic Vital Teeth:
│   ├── Mild: Microabrasion (HCl + pumice) for superficial enamel stains
│   ├── Moderate: Vital bleaching (in-office or night guard)
│   └── Severe: Veneers or crowns after bleaching fails
│
├── Intrinsic Non-Vital Teeth:
│   ├── Walking bleach (sodium perborate) first-line
│   ├── If bleach fails: Internal bleaching with higher-concentration peroxide (in-office)
│   └── Restorative coverage if bleaching unsuccessful or tooth structure compromised
│
└── Prevention:
    ├── Cervical barrier during walking bleach
    ├── White MTA/bioceramics in anterior teeth
    ├── Immediate permanent restoration post-RCT
    └── Patient education on staining foods/medications
```

*Evidence:* Walking bleach achieves 80-90% success for non-vital tooth discoloration; vital bleaching 70-85% for tetracycline stains with multiple applications [[457]]

---

### 4. Classify tooth discolorations & causes. Discuss night guard bleaching tech. - 4m (455, 485-Cons) 📝

**Tooth Discoloration Classification & Causes:**
🔗 *See Question 3 above for comprehensive classification*

**Night Guard Vital Bleaching Technique:**

**Definition:** At-home bleaching using custom-fabricated tray with low-concentration carbamide peroxide gel worn overnight.

**Indications:**
- Mild-moderate intrinsic discoloration in vital teeth
- Patient preference for gradual, controlled lightening
- Cost-effective alternative to in-office bleaching

**Contraindications:**
- Severe tetracycline staining (may require in-office + at-home combination)
- Exposed root surfaces, cervical erosion (risk of sensitivity)
- Pregnancy/lactation (relative contraindication)
- Unrealistic patient expectations

**Materials:**
```
├── Bleaching Agent: 10-15% carbamide peroxide (equivalent to 3.5-5% hydrogen peroxide)
├── Tray Fabrication: 
│   ├── Alginate impression of arch
│   ├── Stone model; vacuum-formed 0.5-1.0mm thick tray
│   └── Trim tray 1mm short of gingival margin to minimize gel contact with gingiva
│
├── Delivery: 
│   ├── Syringe with blunt tip for precise gel placement
│   ├── Instructions: Pea-sized amount per tooth; wipe excess from gingiva
│   └── Storage: Refrigerate gel; discard after 2-4 weeks
```

**Protocol:**
```
1. Pre-Bleaching:
   ├── Comprehensive exam: Rule out caries, cracks, periodontal disease
   ├── Shade documentation: Photos, shade guide recording
   ├── Prophylaxis: Remove extrinsic stains for accurate baseline
   ├── Patient education: Realistic expectations, sensitivity management

2. Tray Fabrication:
   ├── Impressions: Accurate, bubble-free alginate
   ├── Model: Pour immediately; trim to full arch
   ├── Tray: Vacuum-form; trim carefully to avoid gingival impingement
   └── Try-in: Verify fit, comfort; adjust if needed

3. Bleaching Phase:
   ├── Application: Place small amount of gel in tray facial surfaces
   ├── Wear Time: 2-4 hours daytime OR overnight (8 hours); follow manufacturer
   ├── Duration: 2-6 weeks depending on desired shade change
   ├── Monitoring: Weekly check-ins for sensitivity, progress
   └── Sensitivity Management: 
       ├── Potassium nitrate gel in tray pre/post bleaching
       ├── Alternate-day wear if sensitivity occurs
       └── Discontinue if severe sensitivity; resume at lower frequency

4. Completion:
   ├── Final shade assessment: Compare to baseline; document outcome
   ├── Retention: 
   │   ├── "Touch-up" protocol: 1 night every 3-6 months as needed
   │   ├── Avoid staining foods/drinks for 48h post-bleaching
   │   └── Maintain excellent oral hygiene to prolong results
   └── Post-bleaching restoration: Wait 2 weeks before bonding procedures (oxygen inhibition)
```

**Mechanism:** Carbamide peroxide breaks down into hydrogen peroxide + urea; hydrogen peroxide oxidizes chromogenic compounds in enamel/dentin.

**Advantages:**
```
├── Patient Control: Wear at convenient times; adjust frequency based on sensitivity
├── Gradual Results: Natural-looking lightening; reduces rebound effect
├── Cost-Effective: Lower initial cost vs. in-office; reusable trays
├── Safety: Low concentration minimizes sensitivity, gingival irritation
└── Evidence: 70-90% patient satisfaction; shade improvement of 2-8 VITA shades
```

**Limitations & Management:**
```
├── Tooth Sensitivity (Most Common):
│   ├── Cause: Peroxide penetration to pulp; transient inflammation
│   ├── Prevention: Potassium nitrate pre-treatment; lower concentration; shorter wear time
│   └── Management: Desensitizing agents; temporary discontinuation; usually resolves post-treatment
│
├── Gingival Irritation:
│   ├── Cause: Gel contact with soft tissues
│   ├── Prevention: Trim tray 1mm short of gingiva; wipe excess gel
│   └── Management: Topical vitamin E; usually self-limiting
│
├── Uneven Results:
│   ├── Cause: Inconsistent gel application, tray fit issues
│   ├── Prevention: Detailed instructions; tray try-in; monitor progress
│   └── Management: Spot-bleaching with cotton applicator; professional polishing
│
├── Rebound Effect:
│   ├── Cause: Initial dehydration followed by rehydration
│   ├── Prevention: Wait 2 weeks post-bleaching before final shade assessment
│   └── Management: Usually minimal; touch-up bleaching if significant
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
- Success rate: 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration with proper case selection
- Longevity: Results stable 1-3 years; touch-ups maintain shade
- Safety: No evidence of enamel damage with 10-15% carbamide peroxide used as directed

*Clinical Pearl:* Night guard bleaching is first-line for vital tooth discoloration; success depends on patient compliance, realistic expectations, and managing sensitivity proactively [[455]][[485]]

### 5. Vital bleaching of teeth - 4m (463) 📝
🔗 *See Question 4 above for comprehensive night guard bleaching discussion; vital bleaching includes both in-office and at-home techniques*

**Quick 4m Summary:**
- **In-Office:** 25-40% hydrogen peroxide; light/heat activated; 1-3 visits; faster results, higher sensitivity risk
- **At-Home (Night Guard):** 10-15% carbamide peroxide; custom tray; gradual lightening; lower sensitivity
- **Mechanism:** Peroxide oxidizes chromogens in enamel/dentin
- **Indications:** Mild-moderate intrinsic stains in vital teeth; patient preference
- **Contraindications:** Severe tetracycline stains (may need combination), exposed roots, unrealistic expectations
- **Success:** 70-90% achieve 2-8 shade improvement; manage sensitivity with potassium nitrate, gradual protocol

### 6. Hemisection - 2m (447) 📝

**Definition:** Surgical division of a multi-rooted tooth (usually mandibular molar) into two segments; removal of diseased segment, retention and restoration of healthy segment.

**Indications:**
- One root of mandibular molar has vertical fracture, severe resorption, or untreatable canal
- Other root is healthy, restorable, with adequate bone support
- Patient prefers tooth retention over extraction/implant

**Procedure:**
```
1. Pre-op: RCT of entire tooth; ensure good coronal seal
2. Flap: Full-thickness for access to furcation
3. Sectioning: 
   ├── High-speed bur with water coolant to cut tooth through furcation
   ├── Separate roots; remove diseased segment carefully
   └── Contour remaining root: Smooth sharp edges, create emergence profile
4. Closure: Suture flap; allow healing × 4-6 weeks
5. Restoration: 
   ├── Core buildup on retained root
   ├── Crown preparation: Design as premolar (occlusal table reduced)
   └── Full-coverage crown for protection
```

**Prognosis:** 70-80% 5-year survival with proper case selection, oral hygiene, and occlusal management

**Key Success Factors:** Adequate bone support on retained root, patient compliance with oral hygiene, occlusal adjustment to reduce load

*Note: Hemisection converts molar to functional premolar; requires careful treatment planning and patient selection*

---

### 7. Root end filling materials - 2m (439) 📝

**Ideal Properties:**
```
├── Biocompatibility: Non-toxic to periapical tissues
├── Seal: Low microleakage; adapts to dentin
├── Dimensional Stability: No shrinkage after setting
├── Radiopacity: Visible on radiographs
├── Antibacterial: Inhibits residual microbial growth
├── Handling: Workable consistency; adequate setting time
└── Retrievability: Can be removed if retreatment needed
```

**Common Materials:**

| Material | Advantages | Limitations | Clinical Use |
|----------|------------|-------------|-------------|
| **MTA** | Biocompatible, excellent seal, promotes healing, hydrophilic | Long setting time, tooth discoloration (gray), difficult removal | Gold standard for retrofill; anterior teeth use white MTA |
| **Bioceramics** (EndoSequence BC RRM) | Similar to MTA + no discoloration, faster setting, easier handling | Higher cost, less long-term data than MTA | Preferred alternative to MTA; excellent for esthetic zones |
| **Amalgam** | Long history, easy placement, inexpensive | Microleakage, corrosion, mercury concerns, poor biocompatibility | Historical use; largely replaced by MTA/bioceramics |
| **IRM** | Better seal than amalgam, easier removal than MTA | Lower biocompatibility, moderate microleakage | Niche use when MTA contraindicated; interim retrofill |
| **SuperEBA** | Good seal, biocompatible, radiopaque | Technique-sensitive mixing, limited long-term data | Alternative to amalgam; less common than MTA |

**Selection Guidance:**
- MTA/bioceramics first-line for most cases due to superior seal/biocompatibility
- White MTA or bioceramics for anterior teeth to avoid discoloration
- Avoid amalgam in esthetic zones or patients with mercury concerns
- Always place retrofill after thorough retro-preparation (3mm deep, clean walls)

*Evidence:* MTA shows 94% success at 5 years vs. 78% for amalgam in systematic reviews; bioceramics show comparable outcomes with improved handling [[439]]

---

### 8. Carbamide peroxide - 2m (459) 📝

**Definition:** Bleaching agent (CH₆N₂O₃) that breaks down into hydrogen peroxide + urea; used for vital tooth bleaching.

**Properties:**
- Concentration: 10-22% for dental use (10-15% most common for at-home)
- Equivalent H₂O₂: ~3.5-7.5% (carbamide peroxide is ~1/3 strength of H₂O₂)
- pH: ~6.0-7.0 (less acidic than some H₂O₂ formulations)

**Clinical Applications:**
```
├── Night Guard Bleaching: 10-15% in custom trays; worn 2-8 hours/day
├── In-Office Bleaching: Higher concentrations (22-35%) with light activation
├── Walking Bleach (Non-vital): Sometimes used as alternative to sodium perborate
└── Desensitizing: Urea byproduct may help reduce post-bleaching sensitivity
```

**Advantages:**
- Gradual, controlled lightening with lower sensitivity risk vs. high-concentration H₂O₂
- Stable formulation; longer shelf life than some H₂O₂ products
- Urea component may neutralize acidic byproducts, reducing enamel demineralization risk

**Limitations:**
- Slower results than in-office H₂O₂ bleaching
- Requires patient compliance for at-home protocols
- Not ideal for severe tetracycline stains (may need combination approach)

*Note: Carbamide peroxide is gold standard for at-home vital bleaching; success depends on proper case selection and patient management*

---

### 9. Uses of lasers in Endodontics - 2m (476) 📝

**Laser Types & Applications:**

| Laser Type | Wavelength | Primary Endodontic Uses | Evidence Level |
|------------|------------|------------------------|---------------|
| **Er:YAG** | 2940 nm | Dentin ablation, smear layer removal, disinfection | Moderate; effective but expensive |
| **Er,Cr:YSGG** | 2780 nm | Similar to Er:YAG; water-mediated ablation | Moderate; good for irrigation activation |
| **Diode** | 810-980 nm | Soft tissue surgery, bacterial reduction in canals | Strong for soft tissue; limited for intracanal disinfection |
| **Nd:YAG** | 1064 nm | Bacterial reduction, smear layer modification | Moderate; risk of thermal damage if misused |
| **CO₂** | 10,600 nm | Soft tissue incision, hemostasis | Strong for surgery; not for intracanal use |

**Key Applications:**
```
├── Disinfection:
│   ├── Mechanism: Photothermal/photomechanical effects disrupt bacterial cell walls
│   ├── Protocol: Low power (1-2W), short pulses, irrigation between activations
│   └── Evidence: Adjunct to NaOCl; not replacement; modest additional bacterial reduction
│
├── Smear Layer Removal:
│   ├── Mechanism: Laser ablation vaporizes organic/inorganic debris
│   ├── Protocol: Er:YAG/Er,Cr:YSGG with water spray; 50-100mJ/pulse
│   └── Evidence: Comparable to EDTA; technique-sensitive; risk of melting dentin if overdosed
│
├── Soft Tissue Surgery:
│   ├── Mechanism: Precise incision with hemostasis (diode, CO₂)
│   ├── Applications: Flap design, gingivectomy, frenectomy during endo surgery
│   └── Evidence: Superior hemostasis vs. scalpel; faster healing in some studies
│
└── Pain Management:
    ├── Mechanism: Low-level laser therapy (LLLT) reduces inflammation, modulates pain
    ├── Protocol: 810nm diode, 100mW, 30-60 sec per site post-op
    └── Evidence: Moderate support for reducing post-op pain; not universally adopted
```

**Limitations:**
- Cost: High initial investment ($10,000-50,000+)
- Training: Requires specialized education for safe, effective use
- Evidence: Most applications show adjunctive benefit; lasers not replacements for conventional techniques
- Safety: Risk of thermal damage to dentin/PDL if parameters incorrect; mandatory eye protection

*Clinical Takeaway:* Lasers are valuable adjuncts in specific scenarios (soft tissue surgery, irrigation activation) but not essential for routine endodontics; prioritize evidence-based conventional techniques first

### 10. Retrograde filling materials - 2m (439) 📝

- **Gold Standard:** MTA or bioceramics (EndoSequence BC RRM) for biocompatibility, seal, healing promotion
- **Historical:** Amalgam (largely replaced due to microleakage, biocompatibility concerns)
- **Selection:** White MTA/bioceramics for anterior teeth to avoid discoloration; ensure 3mm retro-preparation depth
- **Evidence:** MTA shows 94% success at 5 years vs. 78% for amalgam; bioceramics comparable with improved handling

### 12. Sodium perborate - 2m (459) 📝
🔗 *See Section 2, Question 7 for detailed sodium perborate discussion in walking bleach technique*

**Quick 2m Summary:**
- White crystalline powder releasing hydrogen peroxide slowly; used for intracoronal bleaching of non-vital teeth
- Walking bleach protocol: Mix with water/saline → pack in chamber → seal temporarily → repeat if needed
- Advantages: Safer than high-concentration H₂O₂; lower external cervical resorption risk
- Critical: Always place 2mm cervical barrier (glass ionomer) over GP to prevent bleach diffusion to periodontium
