### 13. Reimplantation - 2m (450) 📝
🔗 *See Section 6, Question 6 for comprehensive avulsion/replantation management*

**Quick 2m Summary:**
- Replantation: Reinserting avulsed tooth into socket; prognosis depends on time out of socket and storage medium
- Ideal: Replant immediately; handle by crown only; rinse gently if dirty (saline/milk)
- Storage: HBSS best; milk acceptable <3 hours; avoid dry storage >60 minutes
- Post-replantation: Splint 7-14 days; antibiotics; RCT for mature teeth within 7-10 days; monitor immature teeth for revascularization

### 14. Enumerate causes of discolouration of teeth & write about treatment options for discoloured anterior teeth - 9m 📝

#### Causes of Tooth Discoloration
🔗 *See Section 7, Question 3 for comprehensive classification*

#### Treatment Options for Discolored Anterior Teeth: Comprehensive Discussion 📝

**I. Extrinsic Stains (Surface):**
```
Management:
├── Professional Prophylaxis:
│   ├── Scaling, polishing with prophylaxis paste
│   ├── Air-polishing (glycine powder) for tenacious stains
│   └── Patient education: Oral hygiene, dietary counseling
│
├── Microabrasion (for superficial enamel stains):
│   ├── Technique: 18% HCl + pumice applied with slow-speed handpiece × 60 sec
│   ├── Indications: Fluorosis, mild tetracycline stains confined to enamel
│   ├── Protocol: Repeat 3-5 times; rinse thoroughly; apply fluoride
│   └── Outcome: Removes 10-100μm enamel; immediate improvement; minimal sensitivity
│
└── Prevention:
    ├── Dietary counseling: Limit coffee, tea, red wine; rinse after consumption
    ├── Tobacco cessation: Critical for long-term stain prevention
    └── Regular maintenance: Professional cleaning every 6 months
```

**II. Intrinsic Stains in Vital Anterior Teeth:**

*A. Mild-Moderate Discoloration (Bleaching First-Line):*
```
1. In-Office Vital Bleaching:
   ├── Agent: 25-40% hydrogen peroxide
   ├── Activation: LED/laser light (controversial efficacy; mainly marketing)
   ├── Protocol: 
   │   ├── Isolate soft tissues with barrier; apply gel to facial surfaces
   │   ├── Activate × 15 min; reapply × 2-3 cycles per visit
   │   ├── Desensitize post-op: Potassium nitrate gel, fluoride varnish
   │   └── Sessions: 1-3 visits × 1 week apart
   │
   ├── Advantages: Immediate results; controlled environment
   ├── Limitations: Higher sensitivity risk; cost; may require touch-ups
   └── Success: 70-85% achieve 3-6 shade improvement

2. At-Home Night Guard Bleaching:
   ├── Agent: 10-15% carbamide peroxide in custom tray
   ├── Protocol: 
   │   ├── Impressions → model → vacuum-formed tray trimmed 1mm short of gingiva
   │   ├── Apply pea-sized gel per tooth; wear 2-8 hours/day × 2-6 weeks
   │   ├── Manage sensitivity: Potassium nitrate pre-treatment; alternate-day wear if needed
   │   └── Monitor progress: Weekly shade checks; adjust protocol as needed
   │
   ├── Advantages: Patient-controlled; lower sensitivity; cost-effective
   ├── Limitations: Slower results; requires compliance
   └── Success: 80-90% achieve 2-8 shade improvement; results stable 1-3 years

3. Combined Approach:
   ├── Protocol: 1 in-office session to jump-start, followed by 2-4 weeks at-home
   ├── Indications: Moderate tetracycline stains, patient desire for faster results
   ├── Outcome: Synergistic effect; higher satisfaction vs. either alone
```

*B. Severe Discoloration (Bleaching-Resistant):*
```
1. Internal/External Bleaching Combination:
   ├── Indication: Teeth with prior trauma/RCT but vital pulp (rare)
   ├── Protocol: Walking bleach + external bleaching; requires careful monitoring
   └── Limitation: Risk of cervical resorption; not first-line

2. Minimally Invasive Restorations:
   ├── Composite Veneers:
   │   ├── Indications: Localized stains, minor shape/size corrections
   │   ├── Technique: Minimal/no-prep; etch, bond, layer composite; polish
   │   ├── Advantages: Conservative, repairable, cost-effective
   │   └── Limitations: Staining over time; 5-7 year lifespan typically
   │
   ├── Porcelain Veneers:
   │   ├── Indications: Severe discoloration unresponsive to bleaching
   │   ├── Technique: 0.3-0.5mm enamel reduction; impression; lab-fabricated veneer; bond with resin cement
   │   ├── Advantages: Superior esthetics, stain resistance, 10-15 year longevity
   │   └── Limitations: Irreversible tooth reduction; higher cost; requires skilled technician
   │
   └── Selection Guide:
       ├── Try bleaching first for all vital teeth; reserve veneers for bleaching failures
       ├── Composite for young patients, budget constraints, minimal correction
       ├── Porcelain for definitive esthetics, long-term solution, patient preference
```

**III. Intrinsic Stains in Non-Vital Anterior Teeth:**
```
1. Walking Bleach (First-Line):
   ├── Protocol: Sodium perborate + water in chamber; cervical barrier essential
   ├── Success: 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration
   └── Limitation: May require 2-4 applications; not for severe stains

2. In-Office Internal Bleaching:
   ├── Agent: 30-35% hydrogen peroxide activated with heat/light
   ├── Protocol: 
   │   ├── Isolate tooth; place cervical barrier; apply gel to chamber
   │   ├── Activate × 15 min; reapply × 2-3 cycles
   │   ├── Seal temporarily; re-evaluate in 1 week; repeat if needed
   │   └── Final restoration: Bonded composite after 2 weeks (oxygen inhibition)
   │
   ├── Advantages: Faster than walking bleach; controlled environment
   ├── Limitations: Higher resorption risk if barrier inadequate; sensitivity possible
   └── Success: 70-85% achieve acceptable shade; lower than walking bleach due to higher concentration risks

3. Restorative Coverage (If Bleaching Fails):
   ├── Composite Buildup + Crown:
   │   ├── Indications: Severe discoloration + structural compromise
   │   ├── Protocol: Core buildup to mask stain; full-coverage crown for protection/esthetics
   │   └── Material choice: All-ceramic for optimal esthetics in anterior zone
   │
   └── Key Principle: Bleaching first; restorative coverage only if bleaching unsuccessful or tooth structure inadequate
```

**IV. Special Considerations for Anterior Teeth:**
```
├── Esthetic Zone Challenges:
│   ├── Shade matching: Critical for anterior restorations; use shade guides + photos
│   ├── Gingival harmony: Margin placement respecting biological width; pink esthetics
│   └── Translucency: Mimic natural enamel; avoid opaque restorations
│
├── Patient Factors:
│   ├── Expectations: Realistic goals; show before/after photos of similar cases
│   ├── Age: Younger patients: prioritize conservative options (bleaching, composite)
│   ├── Budget: Tiered options: bleaching → composite veneer → porcelain veneer
│   └── Compliance: At-home bleaching requires motivation; in-office for less compliant
│
├── Multidisciplinary Approach:
│   ├── Orthodontics first if malpositioned; bleaching/restorations after alignment
│   ├── Periodontal therapy if gingival inflammation; healthy tissues essential for esthetics
│   └── Prosthodontic collaboration for complex cases requiring veneers/crowns
```

**Evidence-Based Treatment Algorithm for Discolored Anterior Teeth:**
```
1. Diagnose etiology: Extrinsic vs. intrinsic; vital vs. non-vital
2. Vital teeth:
   ├── Extrinsic: Prophylaxis → microabrasion if enamel stains
   ├── Intrinsic mild-moderate: Night guard bleaching first-line
   ├── Intrinsic severe: In-office bleaching → composite veneer → porcelain veneer
3. Non-vital teeth:
   ├── Walking bleach first-line (with cervical barrier)
   ├── If fails: In-office internal bleaching → composite crown → porcelain crown
4. Always:
   ├── Address coronal seal first (if non-vital); bleaching fails with microleakage
   ├── Manage sensitivity proactively; potassium nitrate pre/post bleaching
   ├── Document shade changes; set realistic expectations
   └── Prioritize minimally invasive options before irreversible preparations
```

**Prognosis & Long-Term Management:**
```
├── Bleaching: 
│   ├── Results stable 1-3 years; touch-ups maintain shade
│   ├── No evidence of enamel damage with proper protocols
│   └── Avoid staining foods/drinks × 48h post-bleaching
│
├── Veneers:
│   ├── Composite: 5-7 year lifespan; repairable; may stain over time
│   ├── Porcelain: 10-15+ years; highly stain-resistant; irreversible preparation
│   └── Maintenance: Regular hygiene; avoid biting hard objects
│
└── Critical Success Factor: 
    Long-term success depends more on diagnosis, case selection, and patient compliance than specific material choice
```

*Conclusion:* Treatment of discolored anterior teeth requires systematic diagnosis, stepwise minimally invasive approach, and realistic patient communication. Bleaching remains first-line for vital teeth; walking bleach for non-vital; restorative options reserved for bleaching-resistant cases or structural compromise. Multidisciplinary planning and evidence-based material selection optimize esthetic and functional outcomes [[457]][[485]]

---

### 15. Hydrogen Peroxide - 2m (458) 📝

**Hydrogen Peroxide (H₂O₂): Bleaching Agent**

**Properties:**
- Concentration: 3-40% for dental use (3-10% at-home; 25-40% in-office)
- Mechanism: Releases oxygen free radicals that oxidize chromogenic compounds
- Byproducts: Water + oxygen; no residual toxicity

**Clinical Applications:**
```
├── In-Office Vital Bleaching: 25-40% with light/heat activation
├── At-Home Bleaching: 3-10% in custom trays (less common than carbamide peroxide)
├── Non-Vital Bleaching: 30-35% for internal bleaching (walking bleach alternative)
└── Disinfection: 3% solution for canal irrigation (adjunct to NaOCl)
```

**Advantages:**
- Rapid action: Faster results than carbamide peroxide
- Predictable: Well-studied mechanism, concentration-dependent efficacy
- Versatile: Used for vital/non-vital bleaching, disinfection

**Limitations:**
- Sensitivity: Higher concentration → greater risk of post-op sensitivity
- Stability: Degrades with light/heat; requires fresh preparation
- Safety: Can cause chemical burns if contacts soft tissues; requires isolation

**Safety Protocol:**
- Soft tissue isolation: Rubber dam or light-cured barrier for in-office use
- Concentration control: Never exceed recommended levels; dilute if needed
- Patient instructions: Avoid swallowing; rinse immediately if contact with gingiva

*Note: Hydrogen peroxide is gold standard for in-office bleaching; carbamide peroxide preferred for at-home due to slower release, lower sensitivity*

### 16. Root end resection & preparation - 4m (438) 📝
🔗 *See Section 7, Question 2 (Root Resection) for comprehensive apicoectomy protocol including resection and retro-preparation*

**Key Points for 4m Answer:**
- **Resection:** Remove 3mm of apex at 0-10° bevel (minimizes dentinal tubule exposure vs. traditional 45°)
- **Preparation:** Ultrasonic retro-tip creates 3mm deep cavity parallel to long axis; irrigate with EDTA/NaOCl
- **Critical:** Use dental operating microscope for visualization; stain with methylene blue to identify anatomy
- **Retrofill:** MTA/bioceramic condensed into preparation; verify adaptation radiographically
- **Evidence:** Microsurgical technique with 3mm resection + ultrasonic prep + MTA fill achieves 94% success at 5 years

### 17. Define Endodontic Surgery & mention indicans & contraindications. Add a note on investigans - 9m 📝

#### Endodontic Surgery: Definition & Scope

**Definition:** Surgical procedures performed to manage persistent periapical pathology or anatomical complexities not amenable to non-surgical endodontic treatment.

**Types of Endodontic Surgery:**
```
├── Apicoectomy (Root-End Resection): Most common; remove apex + periapical lesion
├── Retrograde Filling: Seal root end after resection
├── Root Amputation: Remove one root of multi-rooted tooth
├── Hemisection: Divide and remove segment of multi-rooted tooth
├── Intentional Replantation: Extract, treat extraorally, replant tooth
├── Perforation Repair: Surgically access and seal iatrogenic/root resorption defects
└── Biopsy: Obtain tissue for histopathological diagnosis
```

#### Indications for Endodontic Surgery

```
I. Persistent Periapical Pathology:
├── Non-healing lesion after adequate non-surgical RCT (confirmed radiographically at 12-24 months)
├── Large periapical cysts unlikely to resolve with RCT alone
└── Symptomatic apical periodontitis unresponsive to non-surgical retreatment

II. Anatomical Complexities:
├── Calcified canals preventing non-surgical access to apex
├── Separated instruments beyond curvature where retrieval not feasible non-surgically
├── Complex root anatomy (C-shaped, dens invaginatus) limiting non-surgical debridement
└── Iatrogenic errors: Perforations, ledges, transportation not correctable non-surgically

III. Diagnostic Needs:
├── Biopsy of periapical lesion to rule out cyst, tumor, non-odontogenic pathology
├── Exploration of suspected vertical root fracture not visible radiographically

IV. Restorative Considerations:
├── Post/core or crown preventing non-surgical access to canal
├── Need for post-space modification not achievable non-surgically

V. Patient Factors:
├── Preference for surgical retention over extraction/implant
├── Medical contraindications to prolonged non-surgical retreatment
```

#### Contraindications

```
Absolute:
├── Poor General Health: Uncontrolled diabetes, immunosuppression, bleeding disorders
├── Inadequate Bone Support: Advanced periodontitis with >50% bone loss around tooth
├── Non-Restorable Tooth: Extensive caries, fracture below bone level, severe root resorption
├── Patient Non-Compliance: Unwilling/unable to follow post-op care, oral hygiene
└── Unrealistic Expectations: Demanding guaranteed success without understanding risks

Relative:
├── Anatomical Challenges: 
│   ├── Proximity to vital structures: Inferior alveolar nerve, maxillary sinus
│   ├── Thin buccal bone: Risk of dehiscence, poor flap closure
│   └── Complex root morphology: Severely curved roots, dilacerations
│
├── Technical Limitations:
│   ├── Inadequate surgical access: Posterior teeth with limited mouth opening
│   ├── Lack of magnification: Microscope essential for modern success rates
│   └── Operator experience: Referral to endodontist if beyond clinician skill
│
├── Alternative Options:
│   ├── Successful non-surgical retreatment possible: Always attempt first
│   ├── Implant therapy preferred: Patient preference, better long-term prognosis
│   └── Cost considerations: Surgery + restoration may exceed extraction + implant cost
│
└── Esthetic Concerns:
    ├── High smile line: Risk of gingival recession affecting esthetics
    ├── Thin gingival biotype: Higher recession risk with flap surgery
    └── Patient priority: Esthetics may favor implant over surgical retention
```

#### Investigations for Endodontic Surgery: Comprehensive Note 📝

**Pre-Surgical Diagnostic Protocol:**

*1. Clinical Assessment:*
```
├── History:
│   ├── Dental: Previous RCT details, symptoms, trauma history
│   ├── Medical: Medications (anticoagulants, bisphosphonates), allergies, systemic conditions
│   └── Social: Smoking status (impairs healing), oral hygiene habits
│
├── Extraoral Examination:
│   ├── Swelling, sinus tracts, lymphadenopathy
│   ├── Facial symmetry, skin changes
│   └── Mouth opening: Assess surgical access feasibility
│
├── Intraoral Examination:
│   ├── Tooth: Mobility, percussion/palpation sensitivity, periodontal probing
│   ├── Restorations: Integrity of coronal seal; need for replacement pre-op
│   ├── Soft tissues: Gingival biotype, attached gingiva width, frenum attachments
│   └── Occlusion: Traumatic contacts; plan for post-op adjustment
```

*2. Radiographic Evaluation:*
```
├── Periapical Radiographs:
│   ├── Multiple angles (0°, +20°, -20°) to assess lesion size, root anatomy
│   ├── Working length verification: Confirm prior RCT adequacy
│   └── Limitations: 2D superimposition; cannot assess bucco-lingual dimensions
│
├── Cone Beam Computed Tomography (CBCT) - Gold Standard:
│   ├── Indications: 
│   │   ├── Complex anatomy (curved roots, C-shaped canals)
│   │   ├── Large lesions requiring precise boundary assessment
│   │   ├── Proximity to vital structures (IAN, sinus, adjacent roots)
│   │   └── Suspected vertical root fracture not visible on 2D
│   │
│   ├── Protocol: Small FOV (5×5 cm); 90μm voxel resolution for endo surgery
│   ├── Analysis: 
│   │   ├── Measure lesion dimensions in 3 planes
│   │   ├── Assess buccal bone thickness (critical for flap design)
│   │   ├── Identify root curvatures, isthmuses, accessory canals
│   │   └── Plan osteotomy size, resection angle, retrofill approach
│   │
│   └── Evidence: CBCT changes surgical plan in 30-60% of cases; improves outcomes
│
├── Supplementary Imaging:
│   ├── Bitewings: Assess interproximal bone levels, caries
│   ├── Panoramic: Screen for gross pathology; not for surgical planning
│   └── Medical CT/MRI: Only if extensive pathology involving jaws/sinuses
```

*3. Diagnostic Tests:*
```
├── Vitality Testing:
│   ├── Confirm non-vital status of surgical tooth
│   ├── Test adjacent teeth to rule out referred pain
│   └── Document baseline for post-op comparison
│
├── Sinus Tract Tracing:
│   ├── Insert GP cone into tract; radiograph to identify source tooth
│   ├── Critical for teeth with multiple possible sources
│   └── Avoid if tract leads to non-odontogenic source (e.g., sinus)
│
├── Periodontal-Probing Correlation:
│   ├── Isolated deep pocket + periapical radiolucency suggests endo-perio lesion
│   ├── Generalized probing depths indicate primary periodontal disease
│   └── Guides whether surgery alone sufficient or combined therapy needed
│
└── Microbiological Sampling (Select Cases):
    ├── Paper point culture from sinus tract or canal
    ├── Indications: Persistent infection despite adequate RCT; immunocompromised patient
    └── Guides antibiotic selection if systemic involvement suspected
```

*4. Risk Assessment & Planning:*
```
├── Surgical Difficulty Index:
│   ├── Tooth location: Anterior (easier) vs. posterior (harder access)
│   ├── Root anatomy: Straight (favorable) vs. curved/dilacerated (challenging)
│   ├── Lesion size: Small (<5mm) vs. large (>10mm; may require grafting)
│   ├── Bone quality: Dense (good healing) vs. osteoporotic (delayed healing)
│   └── Operator experience: Refer complex cases to endodontic specialist
│
├── Medical Risk Stratification:
│   ├── ASA Classification: ASA I-II suitable for office surgery; ASA III+ consider hospital setting
│   ├── Anticoagulation: Coordinate with physician; may continue with local hemostatic measures
│   ├── Bisphosphonates: High risk of ONJ; surgery generally contraindicated
│   └── Diabetes: Optimize HbA1c <8% pre-op; monitor healing closely
│
├── Informed Consent Process:
│   ├── Discuss: Success rates (90-95% for microsurgery), alternatives (extraction/implant), risks (nerve injury, sinus communication, failure)
│   ├── Document: Patient understanding, questions answered, signature
│   └── Realistic expectations: Emphasize healing takes 6-12 months radiographically
```

*5. Pre-Operative Preparations:*
```
├── Medical Optimization:
│   ├── Antibiotic prophylaxis: Only for specific cardiac conditions per AHA guidelines
│   ├── Anticoagulation management: Continue warfarin with INR <3.5; hold DOACs per protocol
│   ├── Glycemic control: HbA1c check for diabetics; optimize pre-op
│   └── Smoking cessation: Advise stopping 2 weeks pre-op to improve healing
│
├── Dental Preparations:
│   ├── Coronal seal: Replace defective restorations pre-op to prevent microleakage
│   ├── Occlusal adjustment: Eliminate traumatic contacts pre-op
│   ├── Oral hygiene: Professional prophylaxis 1 week pre-op; reinforce home care
│   └── Temporary restoration: Ensure adequate seal if final crown delayed
│
├── Surgical Kit Preparation:
│   ├── Magnification: Dental operating microscope with surgical attachments
│   ├── Instruments: Micro-mirrors, ultrasonic retro-tips, MTA carriers, micro-pluggers
│   ├── Materials: MTA/bioceramic retrofill, hemostatic agents (Surgicel), sutures (6-0 monofilament)
│   └── Emergency equipment: Oxygen, epinephrine, resuscitation drugs
```

**Evidence-Based Investigation Guidelines:**
- CBCT is standard of care for surgical planning in complex cases; reduces unexpected findings by 40-60%
- Comprehensive clinical + radiographic assessment improves case selection, success rates by 15-25%
- Multidisciplinary consultation (periodontist, oral surgeon, physician) essential for medically complex patients

*Conclusion:* Thorough pre-surgical investigation is non-negotiable for endodontic surgery success. It enables precise case selection, risk mitigation, surgical planning, and realistic patient communication – transforming surgery from "last resort" to predictable, evidence-based treatment option [[426]]

### 18. Bleaching of Non Vital teeth - 4m (461) 📝
🔗 *See Section 6, Question 2 (Walking Bleach) and Section 7, Question 14 for comprehensive non-vital tooth bleaching protocols*

**Quick 4m Summary:**
- **First-Line:** Walking bleach with sodium perborate + cervical barrier (glass ionomer over GP)
- **Alternative:** In-office internal bleaching with 30-35% H₂O₂ for faster results (higher resorption risk)
- **Critical:** Always place 2mm cervical barrier to prevent bleach diffusion to periodontium (resorption risk)
- **Protocol:** Mix bleach → pack chamber → seal temporarily → re-evaluate in 3-7 days → repeat if needed (max 3-4 applications)
- **Success:** 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration; restorative coverage if bleaching fails
- **Evidence:** Walking bleach safer than high-concentration H₂O₂; cervical barrier reduces external cervical resorption risk to <1%

🔗 *See Section 7, Question 17 for comprehensive indications discussion*

**Quick 2m Summary:**
- Persistent periapical lesion after adequate RCT
- Anatomical complexities preventing non-surgical access (calcifications, separated instruments)
- Diagnostic biopsy needs
- Iatrogenic errors not correctable non-surgically (perforations, transportation)
- Patient preference for surgical retention over extraction/implant
- *Always attempt non-surgical retreatment first when feasible*

**Stain Classification:**

**Vital Bleaching Techniques:**
🔗 *See Section 7, Questions 4 & 5 for detailed night guard and in-office protocols*

**Quick 4m Summary:**
- **In-Office:** 25-40% H₂O₂; light/heat activated; 1-3 visits; faster results, higher sensitivity risk
- **At-Home (Night Guard):** 10-15% carbamide peroxide; custom tray; gradual lightening; lower sensitivity, patient-controlled
- **Combined:** In-office jump-start + at-home maintenance; optimal for moderate stains
- **Mechanism:** Peroxide oxidizes chromogens in enamel/dentin
- **Success Factors:** Case selection, sensitivity management, realistic expectations, patient compliance
- **Evidence:** 70-90% achieve 2-8 shade improvement; results stable 1-3 years with touch-ups

### 21. Hemostatic agents - 4m (433) 📝

**Definition:** Materials used to control bleeding during endodontic surgery, ensuring clear surgical field for retrofill placement.

**Classification & Applications:**

| Agent Type | Examples | Mechanism | Indications | Limitations |
|------------|----------|-----------|-------------|-------------|
| **Mechanical** | Gauze pressure, Surgicel (oxidized cellulose) | Physical barrier + platelet aggregation | Minor oozing; initial hemostasis | Temporary; may interfere with retrofill adhesion if not removed |
| **Chemical** | Epinephrine pellets (1:1000), Aluminum chloride (25%) | Vasoconstriction; protein precipitation | Moderate bleeding; epinephrine for rapid control | Epinephrine: Systemic effects if absorbed; aluminum: Tissue irritation if overused |
| **Biological** | Thrombin, Fibrin sealant | Accelerate clotting cascade | Significant bleeding; patients on anticoagulants | Cost; requires reconstitution; short shelf life |
| **Advanced** | Collagen plugs (CollaPlug), Gelatin sponges (Gelfoam) | Scaffold for clot formation; absorbable | Moderate-severe bleeding; socket preservation | Cost; may require removal if interfering with closure |

**Clinical Protocol for Endodontic Surgery:**
```
1. Prevention First:
   ├── Pre-op: Assess bleeding risk (medications, medical history)
   ├── Anesthesia: Use vasoconstrictor-containing local anesthetic (epinephrine 1:100,000)
   └── Technique: Sharp dissection; minimize tissue trauma; precise osteotomy

2. Intraoperative Hemostasis:
   ├── Step 1: Mechanical pressure with gauze × 2-3 minutes
   ├── Step 2: If persistent: 
   │   ├── Epinephrine pellet: Place on bleeding site × 1-2 minutes; remove before retrofill
   │   ├── Aluminum chloride: Apply on cotton pellet × 30 sec; irrigate thoroughly
   │   └── Oxidized cellulose (Surgicel): Pack into bleeding site; leave in place (absorbable)
   │
   ├── Step 3: For significant bleeding:
   │   ├── Thrombin: Spray or apply on gelatin sponge
   │   ├── Collagen plug: Compress into bleeding socket
   │   └── Fibrin sealant: Apply as liquid; polymerizes in seconds
   │
   └── Critical: Ensure hemostasis BEFORE retrofill placement; blood contamination compromises seal

3. Post-Hemostasis Verification:
   ├── Visual inspection: No active bleeding for 2-3 minutes
   ├── Irrigation test: Gentle saline rinse; confirm no re-bleeding
   └── Proceed to retro-preparation only when field is dry

4. Special Considerations:
   ├── Anticoagulated patients: Coordinate with physician; use mechanical + biological agents; avoid epinephrine pellets
   ├── Hypertensive patients: Limit epinephrine dose; monitor blood pressure
   └── Allergy history: Avoid bovine-derived thrombin in patients with beef allergy
```

**Evidence-Based Recommendations:**
- Epinephrine pellets most effective for rapid hemostasis in healthy patients; remove before retrofill to avoid adhesion interference
- Oxidized cellulose (Surgicel) preferred for absorbable mechanical hemostasis; does not require removal
- For patients on anticoagulants: Mechanical + biological agents (collagen plug + thrombin) safest; avoid epinephrine
- Always irrigate chemical agents thoroughly before retrofill placement to prevent seal compromise

*Clinical Pearl: Hemostasis is not just about stopping blood – it's about creating a clean, dry field for optimal retrofill adaptation; never rush this step*

---

### 22. Classification of Endodontic Surgeries - 4m (432) 📝

**Comprehensive Classification:**

```
I. By Purpose:
├── Therapeutic:
│   ├── Apicoectomy: Remove infected apex + periapical lesion
│   ├── Retrograde filling: Seal root end after resection
│   ├── Root amputation: Remove one root of multi-rooted tooth
│   ├── Hemisection: Divide tooth; remove diseased segment
│   ├── Perforation repair: Seal iatrogenic/root resorption defects
│   └── Intentional replantation: Extract, treat extraorally, replant
│
├── Diagnostic:
│   ├── Biopsy: Obtain tissue for histopathological diagnosis
│   ├── Exploration: Visualize suspected fractures, resorption not visible radiographically
│   └── Culture sampling: Microbiological analysis of persistent infections
│
└── Palliative:
    ├── Incision & drainage: Relieve acute abscess when canal access not possible
    ├── Trephination: Small osteotomy to release pressure in acute apical abscess
    └── Symptom management: Temporary relief while planning definitive care

II. By Anatomical Approach:
├── Anterior Surgery:
│   ├── Maxillary anterior: Access via labial flap; watch for nasopalatine nerve
│   ├── Mandibular anterior: Access via labial flap; mental foramen proximity
│   └── Considerations: Esthetics critical; thin bone; high visibility
│
├── Posterior Surgery:
│   ├── Maxillary posterior: Sinus proximity; palatal flap options
│   ├── Mandibular posterior: Inferior alveolar nerve risk; buccal vs. lingual approach
│   └── Considerations: Access challenging; thicker bone; functional demands
│
└── Multi-rooted Teeth:
    ├── Selective root resection: Amputate one root; retain others
    ├── Furcation management: Debride, fill, or graft furcal defects
    └── Considerations: Complex anatomy; higher technical difficulty

III. By Technique Evolution:
├── Traditional Surgery (Pre-1990s):
│   ├── Large flaps, 45° bevel resection, amalgam retrofill
│   ├── Success: 60-75%; high recurrence due to microleakage
│   └── Limitations: Poor visualization, inadequate seal, tissue trauma
│
├── Modern Microsurgery (1990s-Present):
│   ├── Small flaps, 0-10° bevel, ultrasonic retro-prep, MTA/bioceramic fill
│   ├── Magnification: Dental operating microscope essential
│   ├── Success: 90-95% at 5 years; predictable healing
│   └── Advantages: Minimally invasive, precise, biocompatible materials
│
└── Emerging Techniques:
    ├── Guided surgery: CBCT-based surgical guides for precise osteotomy
    ├── Laser-assisted: Er:YAG for bone cutting; diode for soft tissue
    ├── Regenerative approaches: Scaffolds, growth factors to enhance healing
    └── Status: Investigational; promising but not yet standard of care

IV. By Complexity Level (AAE Surgical Difficulty Index):
├── Level 1 (Simple):
│   ├── Anterior tooth, straight root, small lesion, adequate bone
│   ├── Suitable for general dentist with basic surgical training
│
├── Level 2 (Moderate):
│   ├── Premolar, mild curvature, moderate lesion, some anatomical challenges
│   ├── Requires advanced training; consider referral if inexperienced
│
├── Level 3 (Complex):
│   ├── Molar, severe curvature, large lesion, proximity to vital structures
│   ├── Referral to endodontic specialist strongly recommended
│
└── Level 4 (Highly Complex):
    ├── Medically compromised patient, complex anatomy, revision surgery
    ├── Multidisciplinary planning essential; specialist referral mandatory
```

**Clinical Decision Framework:**
```
1. Confirm non-surgical options exhausted:
   ├── Adequate initial RCT? Coronal seal intact?
   ├── Non-surgical retreatment feasible? Success likelihood?
   └── Patient preference after informed discussion?

2. Assess surgical feasibility:
   ├── Anatomical: Root curvature, bone thickness, vital structure proximity
   ├── Medical: Patient health, medications, healing capacity
   ├── Technical: Operator skill, equipment availability (microscope, CBCT)
   └── Esthetic: Smile line, gingival biotype, patient priorities

3. Select appropriate procedure:
   ├── Apicoectomy: First-line for persistent periapical pathology
   ├── Root amputation/hemisection: Only for multi-rooted teeth with one non-restorable root
   ├── Biopsy: When non-odontogenic pathology suspected
   └── Intentional replantation: Last resort for teeth with no other access

4. Plan with investigations:
   ├── CBCT for complex anatomy; multiple periapicals for simple cases
   ├── Medical consultation if high-risk patient
   └── Multidisciplinary team for combined endo-perio, esthetic zone cases

5. Execute with modern principles:
   ├── Microscope magnification, ultrasonic retro-prep, MTA/bioceramic fill
   ├── Minimally invasive flap design, tension-free closure
   └── Document procedure, materials, post-op instructions
```

*Evidence:* Modern microsurgical techniques achieve 90-95% success vs. 60-75% for traditional surgery; case selection and operator skill are critical success factors beyond technique alone [[432]]
